#5050. Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.

#5045. Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

#5041. Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Gamers in low-income areas will be among the hardest hit by Sony’s decision to stop producing physical game discs from 2028, experts told Decrypt, while one African games industry expert arguing the continent stands to lose out badly.

The secondary market has long been a cheap way for gamers to get hold of games. The PlayStation Store does run sales, but buying digitally assumes a decent internet connection, which rural households are less likely to have.

"By abandoning physical discs, some players at the bottom of the economic spectrum lose access to physical distribution through retail and, more importantly, discounted used sales," Joost van Dreunen, professor at the NYU Stern School of Business and video game business author, told Decrypt. "It won't immediately show up in the financials of major game makers, since their focus is now on creating premium experiences for a luxury class of gamers."

It comes as the cost of being a gamer is trending upwards. Sony raised PS5 prices in March, for example, citing "pressures in the global economic landscape." Similarly, the next generation of Xbox is expected to cost up to $1,800, according to Fortune.

A spokesperson for UK advocacy group Gamer's Voice told Decrypt that Sony's move to cut discs will "likely affect people on lower incomes more."

Isa Muhammad, founder of Africa and Middle East-focused games industry platform Bijetic, told Decrypt that the PS5 is already "out of reach for most Africans." He stressed that those who can buy a console still rely on the second-hand games market to afford to play on the consoles.

Well, at least the highest-income Africans could still take advantage of the PlayStation Store discounts, right? Unfortunately not. Muhammad said that the PlayStation Network is only supported in one African nation: South Africa. Many other nations are missing from the PlayStation Network's official list of supported countries.

"A gamer in Nigeria who wants to buy digitally has to set up an account registered in the U.S. or UK, then pay with foreign gift cards or a dollar card," Muhammad said.

Download speeds in rural, remote, and low-income areas are another major hurdle.

"Bandwidth is now part of what a game costs," van Dreunen said. "Roughly one in four rural [American] households has no wired provider offering 100/20 Mbps, and at 10 Mbps a 50GB download takes about 11 hours."

In Africa and the Middle East, Muhammad said, internet speeds vary significantly across the region—from super-fast connections in Dubai to unreliable ones in Iran. Internet access is also costly in some countries.

"Just recently I paid 75,000 NGN (around $55) for a 450GB broadband bundle, which is more than Nigeria's minimum monthly wage," Muhammad told Decrypt. With GTA 6's pre-load file expected to be 212GB, "a gamer here would first spend $80 on the game, then anywhere from $25 to over $60 just to download it, depending on how they buy data."

And at Nigeria's median download speed of 36.77 Mbps, per Speedtest by Ookla, the download would take nearly 13 hours. Not to mention the fact that they'd need to have a non-Nigerian PlayStation account, a download code corresponding to the country their account is supposedly from, and a way to pay in the local currency.

"Sony's own terms say you must register your account in the country where you live, and Sony can suspend or close accounts created with false information," Muhammad told Decrypt. "So every Nigerian or Egyptian on a US or UK PSN account is breaking the rules from day one."

African gamers live in fear of needing help from Sony and being refused it (or worse, banned) because their accounts break its rules, Muhammad explained. In a future where every purchase is digital, it could open the door to gamers losing accounts that hold every game they've ever purchased.

"If a player in Africa buys GTA 6 and can't recover their account, they may have no choice but to buy it again on a new one, along with everything else they've lost," he said. "A disc-free future will see this become the reality for every new PlayStation game, for gamers across most of Africa."

Sony and PlayStation did not respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

All accounts will be closed on Dec. 31, and any assets left on the exchange will be charged monthly fees starting on Nov. 29, Cryptoast, Crypto Briefing and other media reported on Saturday, citing emails sent to users by Binance.

On that date, accounts still maintaining a balance will pay a monthly maintenance fee of 0.025% of the balance, or a minimum of 10 USDC, a stablecoin tracking the US dollar.

To avoid fees, withdrawals must be completed by Nov. 29, the exchange said.

A Binance email cited by Cryptoast said: “Due to the orderly cessation of activities, your Binance France account will close as of December 31, 2026. If you have not withdrawn your crypto-assets as of December 31, 2026, this message constitutes a notification of our intention to consider your account inactive, in accordance with our Terms of Use. This can result in the conversion, transfer or other processing of your crypto-assets.”

On Sunday, a Binance spokesperson responded to a Cointelegraph query, saying, “Following the end of the MiCA transition period, Binance is progressively restricting certain services for affected users, in line with the schedule previously communicated to them.”

Though asked specifically about reports similar emails had also been sent to users in Spain and Belgium, the spokesperson made no mention of them.

#5037. AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI stocks staged a comeback Friday after a rout that swept from Wall Street to Asia. But a single green session hardly settles the bigger question for investors heading into the weekend: Is this an overdue correction, or the beginning of an AI bubble bursting?

Nvidia rose 2.78% to $186.64, after plunging 9.3% this Thursday. AMD also bounced to $449.04 following a 17.3% collapse a day earlier. Both stocks remain well below their October peaks, showing how little a sharp relief rally can repair.

It wasn’t just U.S. markets seeing red either. South Korea’s KOSPI lost 6% Friday, while Taiwan’s benchmark dropped more than 8% during the week—its worst week in months. TSMC hit its daily limit-down threshold during the rout. Reuters reported that Taiwanese officials asked state-owned banks to help support prices.

The US Tech 100 cash-market proxy also recovered, gaining 1.85% to 27,007.3, which means it’s down more than 5.5% this week. Its daily candles show the bounce arriving after a much larger slide from October levels, not a return to the previous trend.

The U.S. midterms accelerated fears that Democratic gains would mean tougher scrutiny of data centers. In 40 of 41 local ballot contests on November 3, voters favored limiting development, including by rejecting pro-construction measures, Ballotpedia found. Some votes were advisory, but the political message was unmistakable.

That matters for chip companies because data centers are their biggest growth story. Each project requires vast spending on processors, servers, power and cooling. Restrictions could delay orders or force construction elsewhere.

Yet the doubts predated the election. AI companies and their customers are spending aggressively to build capacity, often before returns are clear. More expensive borrowing makes that bet harder to sustain. Reuters quoted market strategist Steve Sosnick warning that heavily owned stocks were already “priced to perfection.”

A correction means a market has fallen at least 10% from a recent peak. It does not necessarily mean the underlying business is shrinking. Imagine a chipmaker whose shares trade at 60 times annual earnings because investors expect explosive growth. If investors become willing to pay only 40 times those same earnings, the stock can lose a third of its value without profits falling at all.

A bubble bursting is more serious: It happens when expectations prove unrealistic and the money supporting the boom begins to disappear.

History offers a useful warning. During the dot-com crash, the Nasdaq lost roughly 77% between March 2000 and October 2002. The internet was not a fad, but investors had vastly overestimated how quickly companies could turn it into profit.

Telecom operators spent fortunes laying fiber-optic cables for customers who had yet to arrive. Global Crossing, which built an international network, filed for bankruptcy in 2002. The technology survived; many companies and shareholders did not.

AI could face a similar mismatch between long-term promise and near-term returns—and some investors are already talking about it. For example, the RAM shortage is also a business opportunity that many companies are trying to use for their advantage. Former crypto miners are renting their hardware and cloud provider and inference startups are appearing everywhere.

Now, consider a hypothetical company borrowing billions to build a data center packed with Nvidia chips, expecting to rent that computing power at premium prices for years. If customers spend less than anticipated, borrowing becomes more expensive or construction is delayed, the debt still has to be repaid.

The operator may cancel chip orders, its contractors lose work, and utilities that planned new capacity lose anticipated customers. Lenders and investors holding its debt could also take losses. A stock-market selloff can then become a problem for the wider economy.

There is an important difference from 2000, however. Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon and Meta are profitable businesses with established customers and substantial cash flows, unlike many of the speculative dot-com startups. They can withstand setbacks better than smaller AI-cloud providers that depend on repeatedly borrowing to expand.

It is possible for AI to keep transforming industries while a bunch of individual AI companies fail—and for investors in successful companies to have overpaid.

The financial warning lights are beginning to flicker. Reuters reported Friday that the cost of insuring against a default by AI-cloud provider CoreWeave had climbed above 1,000 basis points. Such protection works somewhat like insurance on a loan: as the price rises, investors are signaling greater concern about repayment.

Yet broader bond markets were still operating relatively normally. That points to growing strain in the riskiest corners of AI financing, not proof of a system-wide crisis.

Next week will offer more concrete tests than another day of red or green candles. Results from CoreWeave, Applied Materials, and Cisco could reveal whether customers are still ordering capacity and equipment, while inflation data will influence expectations for borrowing costs. Stable orders and manageable debt would support the correction argument.

Repeated canceled projects, failed refinancing and shrinking sales would suggest the boom's financial foundations are cracking. The question is not whether AI has a future, but whether every company betting billions on that future can afford to get there.

#5030. Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

Every member of Wall Street’s “Magnificent Seven” now wants its stock kept off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Apple and Meta Platforms are among 11 companies added to the list of objecting issuers in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Thursday.

They join Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon, Nvidia and Tesla, completing a sweep by the group of tech giants that makes up a large share of the S&P 500’s value. The other new objectors are Home Depot, Boeing, GE Vernova, Palantir, ServiceNow, S&P Global, KLA, Super Micro Computer and Nebius Group, bringing the total number of companies saying “no” to the OKX and ICE platform to 67.

Meta’s objection carries some irony. The Facebook parent once tried to launch its own stablecoin, Libra, later renamed Diem, before abandoning the project in 2022 amid regulatory pushback.

Apple had been one of the last megacaps missing from the list after Amazon, Tesla, and SpaceX objected in the venue’s Nov. 2 notice. The tally stood at 56 then, and at nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

Just as notable is who hasn’t objected. Crypto-friendly public companies such as Coinbase, Robinhood and Strategy don’t appear on the list. Coinbase launched tokenized stocks for non-U.S. users on its Base network in August. Berkshire Hathaway, Visa, and Exxon Mobil are also absent. It’s unclear whether OKXICE notified those companies.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act failed to advance in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. It’s worth noting, though, that the objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain. An outside “tokenizer” would hold real shares one-for-one through a registered broker-dealer.

With the market’s biggest names opted out, it’s unclear which major stocks the venue will be able to offer at launch, and its notices don’t list the tokens it plans to make available.

#5028. This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

Bitcoin developer Cory Fields has an unusual idea for dealing with future quantum thieves: Make them risk their own Bitcoin every time they try to steal someone else’s.

In a November 4 post to the Bitcoin development mailing list, Fields outlined “quantum landmines” and two types of auctions designed to turn attacks on vulnerable wallets into expensive gambles. He called the ideas “thought experiments,” not a completed Bitcoin upgrade.

Bitcoin owners use a secret private key to authorize transactions. A matching public key lets the network check their signatures without learning the secret.

If the whole idea seems too hard to understand, we used AI to generate a video to help you grasp it:

Today’s computers cannot realistically calculate that private key from the public one. A sufficiently powerful quantum computer could use a mathematical technique called Shor’s algorithm to do it, potentially letting a thief spend coins as if they owned them.

Some older Bitcoin outputs have exposed public keys, and certain newer formats reveal them too. Other addresses hide the key until someone spends, so the risk is not identical for every wallet.

Researchers call the hypothetical arrival of a computer capable of these attacks “Q-Day.” No publicly demonstrated machine can steal Bitcoin this way today, and nobody knows exactly when one might exist.

Imagine someone owns 1 BTC in an old, potentially vulnerable wallet. Before Q-Day, this person creates a tiny, separate quantum-safe Bitcoin output containing a hidden proof that they controlled the original private key while quantum theft was still impossible.

Think of it as a sealed ownership receipt. Outsiders can see the small output, but not the evidence tucked inside it, or even that it is a trap.

Now imagine a future thief uses a quantum computer to recover the user’s old private key. Under Fields’s suggested rules, the thief cannot simply take her 1 BTC: They must add 1 BTC of their own from a quantum-safe wallet as collateral.

Both coins enter a restricted output worth 2 BTC. The thief must wait through a challenge period—30 days in Fields’s example—before spending it freely.

The wallet owner notices the transaction and reveals the pre-Q-Day proof. The network then allows the owner to move the full 2 BTC to the quantum-safe address they committed to earlier: their original coin, plus the thief’s collateral.

The attacker has no way to know in advance which old wallets contain these hidden receipts. Even a successful quantum key recovery could become a 1 BTC loss.

The wallet owner would also need matching collateral to initiate a normal post-Q-Day move of her own legacy coin. If they have no spare BTC, the same rule intended to hurt thieves could make moving her savings difficult.

Landmines only work when an owner prepares one before Q-Day and reveals it in time. They cannot protect coins whose keys are already lost, including some of the early Bitcoin holdings widely believed to be inaccessible.

Fields proposes a second mechanism called a staked auction. Before Q-Day, someone could lock up quantum-safe Bitcoin and secretly register a bid against a specific old wallet they expect thieves to target.

Suppose an apparently abandoned wallet holds 50 BTC. A future attacker tries to move it and posts another 50 BTC as collateral; during the auction period, someone who committed a bid in advance can challenge the attempted theft.

In Fields’s example, a successful challenger claims part of the attacker’s collateral while the original vulnerable coins are destroyed rather than awarded to the bidder. The point is to deprive thieves of a reliable payday, not return the coins to a missing owner.

His third idea, a sats auction, requires no advance stake. Instead, a vulnerable-coin spend would trigger a contest in which the highest bidder gets the coins minus their bid, while the runner-up pays their bid and gets nothing.

That resembles a notorious auction game where two bidders keep raising their offers because neither wants to be the person who loses money for nothing. A thief might avoid starting a contest that could cost more than the coins they hoped to steal.

One proposed response to quantum risk is to restrict spending from outdated wallets after a migration deadline. That might prevent theft, but could also permanently lock out legitimate owners who never moved their coins.

Fields is exploring a different trade-off: Keep old coins potentially spendable, but make attempted transfers costly and open to challenges. Bitcoin cannot tell an honest owner from a quantum thief if both can produce the same valid signature.

The trade-off is severe, considering that honest owners might need large collateral deposits, inactive holders could miss their chance to object, and auction rules might burn coins belonging to people who are still alive and simply offline.

Bitcoin would first need a quantum-safe way to hold and spend funds. Wallets would then need to support hidden ownership commitments, while Bitcoin’s consensus rules would have to enforce collateral, waiting periods, special recovery transactions, and auctions.

Developers would also need to agree on when the new rules activate, how much collateral is required, and what happens to losing bids or leftover funds. Fields believes some mechanics could be introduced through a soft fork, a type of Bitcoin rule change, but has not supplied the detailed design.

None of these protections exists in Bitcoin today. Fields has published no implementation or activation schedule, and his original post leaves the auction payouts and even the desired outcome of an auction unresolved.

#5027. Vitalik ‘pivots’ ETH to store of value, crypto suffers after US midterms: Hodler’s Digest

Vitalik Buterin wants Ethereum to embrace a store of value narrative, upsetting Bitcoin maxis. The crypto industry faces an uncertain future following the US elections.

Ethereum creator Vitalik Buterin says the network should promote ETH as a store of value, rather than as an asset that derives value from burning fees.

He argued in a thread that idea of “ETH as fee-burning token never was as strong as it seemed” and that optimizing for that would negatively impact Ethereum’s “decentralization and capture resistance.”

The 2021 EIP-1559 fee burning upgrade flipped Ethereum into deflationary mode for a time when fee revenue was sky high, leading to supporters, including the Bankless podcasters, touting Ethereum as “ultrasound money.”

But due to design choices, fees have kept falling along with revenue, making that narrative unsustainable. From January to October this year median transaction fees fell from 35 cents to 3.5 cents, even as the number of daily transactions increased by more than half.

Some Bitcoiners, who hold BTC’s store of value status sacred, were scathing about Buterin’s thread. Held Media founder Dan Held derided Buterin’s comments as, “ETH trying to pivot to Bitcoin’s SoV narrative after all of its previous narratives collapsed. Again.” He argued it was “wild that you think a protocol with a constantly changing monetary policy (just like fiat) can be a ‘sound money.’” However, proponents note that Ethereum has not changed its monetary policy since The Merge in 2022, and each change has reduced, rather than increased, issuance.

Ethereum maxis welcomed the protocol’s creator setting a clear narrative direction. MetaLeX attorney Gabriel Shapiro called it a “nature is healing moment” from Buterin, and said, “I think on fundamentals ETH can be a much better SoV than both ZEC and BTC.”

Even Solana co-founder Anatoly Yakovenko said Buterin was “absolutely right that you can’t focus on fees as the product” although he then took a subtle dig at Ethereum’s “product.”

Despite spending $176 million across 100 seats — including $64 million trying and failing to keep Sherrod Brown out of the Senate — the influence of crypto super PACS on the election appeared to be minimal. Or was it?

Even though the Democrats now control both the Senate and the House of Representatives a Fairshake spokeman told Crypto In America the super PAC had spent $154.5 million across 93 races and backed the winning candidate in all but seven races. That’s a 92% success rate.

Stand With Crypto notes that a majority of legislators in both houses is now pro-crypto, with 50 of 100 senators designated “pro-crypto” and only 33 “anti-crypto,” while 241 congress people support the industry, and only 106 actively hate it.

However the anti-crypto army will slither into positions of power, with Brown likely to join the Senate Agriculture Committee and Elizabeth Warren tipped to chair the Senate Banking Committee.

“The next Congress will bring a fundamentally different environment for crypto in Washington, and the industry should expect significantly more scrutiny,” said Kyle Bligen, executive director at the Decentralization Research Center.Democrats are already reportedly considering expanding proposals from Representative Maxine Waters into a broader market structure bill. There is still an outside chance the CLARITY Act could be resurrected, but only if it has much stronger ethics provisions aimed at President Donald Trump’s alleged corruption.

“A sitting president’s family launching memecoins and a stablecoin is a real governance problem,” Fidelity Digital founder Hadley Stern told Magazine. “If I’d done that at Fidelity, I’d have been walked out the same afternoon.”

The TRUMP memecoin lost a third of its value this week, and was the biggest loser in the top 100 cryptocurrencies.

The election result saw AI related stocks crash and Bitcoin fell below $76,000 where it remains.

While the exchange quickly detected the incident, the hackers had already obtained information about employees working on Binance’s wallet infrastructure.

In September, attackers posing as recruiters targeted another software engineer and tricked the employee into downloading a malicious file disguised as a coding assignment.

On Oct. 20, the attackers used the compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain access to two additional engineers’ systems to deploy malware. They then gained access to the exchange’s internal wallet-signing service, enabling them to steal billions.

“This wasn’t one mistake or one breach — it was a months-long campaign,” Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said in a Tuesday post on X.

Ethereum Foundation researcher Justin Drake is targeting December 2028 to complete Ethereum’s post-quantum and post-AI transition, a year earlier than the foundation’s previously announced quantum-resistance deadline.

Speaking at Devcon 8 in Mumbai on Tuesday, Drake outlined the accelerated timeline. “Our hope is that by December 2028 we’ll be able to wrap up the full post-quantum and post-AI transition,” Drake said, adding that developers have 24 months to deliver three additional network upgrades.

The Ethereum Foundation’s September roadmap established December 2029 as its deadline for achieving quantum resistance.

However the roadmap is currently on hold, with core developers delaying the Glamsterdam upgrade indefinitely until security concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants are resolved.

More would have undoubtedly gone through, but trading on the cross chain swaps platform was halted automatically due to an unrelated Ethereum block reorganization.

Co-founder JPThor said the fact that the FBI warning had no effect on the DEX proved that THORChain was decentralized.

At the end of the week, Bitcoin (BTC) is down 4.5% to trade at $74,302, Ethereum (ETH) is down 6.3% to trade at $2,117 and XRP (XRP) is down 5% to $1.11. The total market cap is at $2.49 trillion according to CoinMarketCap.

The top three altcoin losers of the week are OFFICIAL TRUMP which was down 32.8%, Worldcoin (WLD) down 20.8% and Ethena (ENA) down 19%.

#5026. This Is Not Your Ordinary Flu Season

The CDC released new data that changes what to expect this season.

EVERY FLU SEASON is different. Some come earlier, others later. Some are mild, while others—like last year—barrel through like a tornado. Every year, scientists do their best to predict what type of influenza virus will hit the US based on data from countries in the Southern Hemisphere whose flu seasons come earlier than ours.

The key word here is predict. Since the influenza virus mutates quickly, it’s hard for scientists to give a 100 percent guarantee that what’s ravaging Australia will also come for the US. That’s what happened in the brutal flu season last year—and this year.

A few weeks ago, scientists gave a forecast of H3N2 (that’s the one dubbed “Super-K”) as the dominant flu strain for the US. But early surveillance in flu cases from the West Coast found that a different type—Influenza A virus subtype H1N1—has overtaken H3N2 as the dominant flu strain in October. The CDC also released new flu data reporting a spike in flu-related hospitalizations.

Does H1N1 sound eerily familiar? H1N1 is also known as swine flu—a strain that sparked a pandemic back in 2009. So far, researchers have linked early flu ER visits to a mutation in H1N1 (a G155E mutation) that changes the way the virus attaches to and infects cells. Past research has found antibodies from past H1N1 infections or vaccinations may not recognize it, especially in younger populations. In other words, there is a chance the mutated influenza virus could evade some pre-existing immunity.

Here is what you need to know about this early jump to flu season and how to best protect yourself against this mutated strain.

HERE’S WHAT THE CDC learned: In mid-October this year, the agency saw 10 times more hospitalizations for flu than it did in 2025 (2.1 hospitalizations per 100,000 people compared to 0.2 per 100,000 during the same week last year).

Among other changes are when and where the virus is spreading. While influenza cases typically tick up around fall, infections began rising in August. The boost of early cases was mostly on the West Coast, including Arizona, Nevada, and California. Hawaii and Louisiana are already at moderate levels. Typically, the US flu season starts in the South.

Flu activity remains low nationally. But as of October 28, the CDC estimates that infections are growing or likely growing in 39 states. The numbers coincide with early reports of flu activity ramping up on the East Coast in states including Pennsylvania and New York.

WHILE SCIENTISTS INITIALLY predicted a “Super-K” flu strain to be dominant, this year’s vaccine accounts for multiple flu strains. The current flu shots will target three strains: H1N1, B/Victoria lineage, and H3N2. It’s too soon to see if the vaccine will fully protect against the mutated H1N1 strain, but it’s still expected to provide some meaningful protection.

“A mismatch is not a black-or-white situation. We may have less immunity, not no immunity,” Andrew Pekosz, PhD, virologist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health told TODAY.com. You can also take some reassurance from last year when people who still got the mismatched flu shot were still less likely to have severe illness and be hospitalized than those who were unvaccinated.

ALONG WITH YOUR traditional flu shot, adults ages 50 and over are eligible for a new flu vaccine called mFLUSIVA made with the same mRNA technology as the COVID vaccines. A phase 3 clinical trial on the mFLUSIVA vaccine found it was more effective at preventing illness from influenza virus subtypes A and B than a standard vaccine. Reminder, the dominant strain right now is Influenza A virus subtype H1N1.

Of the 20,179 people injected with the new shot, only 411 developed a lab-confirmed case (2 percent) 14 days after vaccination during flu season last year. Meanwhile, 557 of 20,124 recipients of the standard vaccine got sick with the flu (2.8 percent).

One caveat, however, was that the new flu vaccine has a greater chance of severe side effects. In the clinical trial, people were more likely to report injection site pain, fatigue, headaches, and muscle weakness.

Don’t want a shot? That’s fine! You also have your choice of a nasal flu vaccine a.k.a. FluMist if you’re ages 2 to 49. “We tend to develop less of an immune response in the nose as we get older, which means the nasal spray vaccine doesn’t work as well in older groups,” John Sellick, DO, infectious disease expert and professor of medicine at the University at Buffalo, previously told MH.

Ultimately, time isn’t running out yet to get yourself armed and ready for the US flu season. The influenza virus is fast and mutates easily, but vaccination remains your best option to weather the storm.

Jocelyn Solis-Moreira, MS is the associate health & fitness for Men's Health and has previously written for CNN, Scientific American, Popular Science, and National Geographic before joining the brand. When she's not working, she's doing circus arts or working towards the perfect pull-up.

#5025. Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

Nvidia's board of directors has opened an independent investigation following reports that CEO Jensen Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort, the company said on Sunday.

A committee of independent directors, assisted by outside counsel, will oversee the inquiry. "While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," it said, adding that it "has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team."

We just issued a press release from our board of directors. Read the release: https://t.co/W9yXWJzbkk

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 8, 2026

The investigation follows a Financial Times report on Saturday that a U.S. government memo, written earlier this year based on intelligence information, said the Nvidia CEO had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed, citing people familiar with the memo. U.S. intelligence believes she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security, the FT reported. Nvidia declined to comment to the newspaper on the memo. Huang's email to staff, sent on Sunday, did not name the person with whom he had an affair.

Lawmakers from both parties are seeking answers. Republican leaders have asked Huang to appear privately before the House China, foreign affairs and intelligence committees, according to the FT, while Sen. Chris Coons (D-DE), who will chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee from January, pledged in a tweet that "Congress will investigate and get answers."

The FT's report built on a New York Times investigation last month, which said U.S. intelligence agencies had concluded that Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, a Chinese citizen, was a Chinese intelligence asset, and that surveillance of her had revealed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. President Donald Trump was briefed on the findings roughly a year ago and chose not to act on them, according to the Times.

U.S. officials suspected Megaspeed of helping Chinese companies get around restrictions on advanced AI chips, and the Commerce Department's investigation into the company has been handed to the Justice Department, the Times reported. Megaspeed's sister company in Malaysia bought at least $4.8 billion worth of AI equipment containing Nvidia chips between June 2024 and February 2026, according to trade records cited by the newspaper.

Nvidia told the Times that Huang's interactions with Ms. Huang were "not unusual or unexpected," and that customers like Megaspeed receive "ordinary business information." Megaspeed said it had retained outside counsel to conduct an internal investigation, Ms. Huang did not respond to the newspaper's requests for comment, and China's embassy in Washington dismissed the allegations as "groundless smears and slanders."

In an email to employees on Sunday, Huang apologized to his family and colleagues. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," he wrote, according to a copy published by CNBC.

"Today's news will not slow NVIDIA. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead NVIDIA as CEO without interruption," the company told CNBC.

Nvidia shares were down 5% at $177.32 in premarket trading early on Monday, after closing at $186.64 on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance.

#5024. Trump Says He Knows 'Nothing' About World Liberty Financial as He Defends UAE Ties

Trump Says He Knows 'Nothing' About World Liberty Financial as He Defends UAE Ties

U.S. President Donald Trump has defended his family's stake in crypto venture World Liberty Financial, saying he knows "nothing" about the firm despite being a co-owner, in an interview after last week's midterm elections.

Asked by columnist Ross Douthat why his co-ownership of a company partly owned by investors linked to the United Arab Emirates government was different from the kind of arrangement he would call corrupt if it involved the Biden family, Trump replied: "Here's the difference [... ] I'm gonna tell you why. Because I know nothing about it," according to a transcript published by CBS News.

Pressed on why he did not simply divest, Trump said most of his money is managed by Wall Street firms through index funds, and that his sons run the family business. "It's hard to say to them, 'Don't do anything anymore for four years because I'm president,'" he said.

When Douthat asked whether foreign investors in the company believe they are buying influence, Trump said: "I don't know." He argued that conflicts of interest have dogged presidents "going back to George Washington," adding that past presidents "own something, whatever crypto was at the time."

"There's crypto, and before that there was oil," Trump said, adding that, "every president has had this problem."

"I'm a bigger name than other presidents, whether we like it or not," Trump said.

World Liberty Financial, which Eric Trump announced in August 2024, issues the WLFI governance token and the USD1 stablecoin. In January, it launched World Liberty Markets, a lending and borrowing platform built on DeFi protocol Dolomite.

Its UAE links have drawn scrutiny from Democrats. Aryam Investment, a UAE-backed entity tied to the country's national security adviser, Sheikh Tahnoon bin Zayed, agreed to buy a 49% stake in World Liberty Financial for $500 million four days before Trump's January 2025 inauguration, Decrypt reported in February, citing The Wall Street Journal. The deal, signed by Eric Trump on World Liberty's behalf, reportedly preceded the administration's reversal of restrictions on advanced AI chip sales to the UAE. A World Liberty spokesperson said at the time that neither Trump nor his Middle East envoy, Steve Witkoff, had "any involvement whatsoever" in the transaction, ABC News reported. Sen. Chris Murphy (D-CT) described the arrangement in February as "potentially criminal conduct." Trump has previously said he was not aware of the deal.

The company's UAE ties have also surfaced in its legal battle with Tron founder Justin Sun. Sun's lawyers asked a federal court to sanction World Liberty, alleging the firm delayed disclosing UAE ownership that could defeat federal jurisdiction over his lawsuit. World Liberty has called Sun's legal campaign a "meritless PR stunt."

The Trump family's financial stake in the venture is set out in World Liberty's own disclosures. DT Marks DEFI LLC, an entity affiliated with Trump and certain family members, owns about 38% of the holding company behind World Liberty Financial, holds 22.5 billion WLFI tokens, and is entitled to 75% of the net proceeds from WLFI token sales, according to the company. A separate Trump-affiliated entity has an indirect claim on interest earned on the reserves backing USD1. The disclosures state that no member of the Trump family is an officer, director or employee of World Liberty, though Trump's sons have remained involved with the company, Decrypt reported earlier this year.

#5023. The 2027 Kia EV9 costs $90 more, but at least you get a few extras

Kia barely touched the 2027 EV9’s price, raising it by only $90 while sneaking in a few extra standard features on select trims.

Kia’s three-row electric SUV returns for the 2027 model year, mostly as a carryover model with a few design and packaging updates.

The 2027 Kia EV9 starts at $54,990, up a measly $90 from last year’s model. That’s for the base EV9 Light short-range trim, which uses a 76.1 kWh battery and delivers up to 230 miles of range.

Higher-priced trims, including the Light Long Range, Wind, Land, and GT-Line, use a larger 99.8 kWh battery that delivers up to 305 miles of range in RWD variants.

The Wind and Land trims have standard all-wheel-drive (AWD) and offer up to 283 miles of range, while the GT-Line is also AWD but offers slightly less range at 280 miles.

Kia’s dual-motor e-AWD system delivers up to 379 hp and 516 lb-ft of torque in the EV9 GT-Line, enabling it to accelerate from 0 to 60 mph in 4.5 seconds.

For the 2027 model, the EV9 Wind now comes with black-finish 19″ wheels and the Glossy Black exterior paint color as standard, and Wolf Gray paint is newly available as an option.

The 2027 EV9 Land now includes the Nightfall Edition Package, which was previously optional, as standard. It starts at $69,990, or $500 less than the 2026 Kia EV9 Land Nightfall Edition.

The package includes a considerable performance boost with torque rising from 379 lb-ft to 516 lb-ft, plus an exclusive black interior and exterior design elements.

Inside, the 2027 EV9 features Kia’s Digital Cockpit with nearly 30″ of combined displays. The setup includes dual 12.3″ instrument displays and navigation screens in a curved panoramic display, plus a 5″ climate control screen.

Kia’s three-row electric SUV offers up to 42.8″ of legroom in the second row, which is even more than the 2025 Cadillac Escalade. It’s available in six- and seven-seat configurations.

The EV9 is 197.2″ long, 77.9″ wide, and 68.9″ tall, with a 122″ wheelbase, or slightly smaller than the Rivian R1S and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

With DC fast charging of up to 350 kW, the 2027 Kia EV9’s battery can charge from 10% to 80% in as little as 24 minutes.

All 2027 Kia EV9s also come with a built-in NACS charge port, so you can use Tesla Superchargers without a separate adaptor.

#5022. 10 Best Sweatpants for Women, Tested By Fashion Experts

Our favorite pair on this list is from Vuori.

After testing more than 50 sweatpants over the past two years, I can confidently say today’s best sweatpants for women are no longer reserved for bed rotting on lazy Sundays. I’ve worn top-rated styles with a white t-shirts, styled them with my go-to wool coat for running errands, and paired them with an oversized hoodie while WFH. The difference between frumpy and fashionable comes down to fabric, fit, and style: today’s standouts are made with soft, stretchy, and moisture-wicking materials that are a cinch to move in and hold their shape well. And thanks to activewear all-stars like lululemon and Alo Yoga, modern sweats feel polished enough to wear beyond the couch—without sacrificing comfort.

To find the best sweats, the WH fashion team did the legwork (get it?) for you, trying tons of popular pairs on a variety of body types. We landed on oversized, fuzzy styles made for couch hangs to sweat-wicking joggers that can handle anything your day throws at you. Keep reading to find out what to consider when shopping—and which pairs deserve a spot in your cozy drawer.

I have these in both the jogger and wide-leg versions, and both are in my regular rotation all fall and winter. Plus, they have such a flattering drape that doesn’t emphasize my hips or belly. “It has that classic old school sweatpant texture that’s soft and thick,” says Saladino.

Softness is a must when it comes to sweatpants, and stylist Jackie Schiavone says the right material is key.

Sweatpants are meant to be oversized, so you don’t have to worry as much about fit, as, say, jeans. But it’s important to make sure the pair you choose is still flattering. Look for a body-skimming fit that doesn’t cling too tightly to your thighs while sitting comfortably at your waist or hips. Also, pay attention to length. You don’t want your pant leg to drag on the ground while you walk, so consider options that come in petite, regular, and tall lengths, depending on your height.

#5021. tBTC bridge partially resumes as $120M Aave reserve remains frozen

Threshold halted its tBTC bridge on Oct. 30 as operators addressed a critical Linux kernel vulnerability that could allow attackers to execute code on unpatched servers.

Threshold Network’s tBTC bridge has resumed onchain operations after a nine-day security pause, but an Aave reserve holding about $120 million in tBTC remains frozen to new deposits and borrowing against the token.

On Sunday, Threshold said the bridge’s underlying network was restarting as security-patched node operators reconnected. These independent operators collectively authorize Bitcoin movements and process deposits and redemptions.

Requests submitted before the shutdown are expected to be processed as more operators return, but the app will not accept new deposits, minting or redemptions until enough nodes are online. Threshold did not announce a restart date.

Threshold initially halted the bridge on Oct. 30 while operators addressed a critical Linux kernel vulnerability that could allow remote attackers to execute code on unpatched servers. The project said it found no evidence the flaw was exploited against tBTC nodes.

Aave froze its tBTC reserve on Ethereum Core on Oct. 31 to prevent additional exposure while redemptions were unavailable. The restriction stops new tBTC deposits and the token from adding borrowing power, while existing users can still withdraw and repay loans.

Threshold said roughly 4,320 BTC remains in bridge wallets against about 4,185 tBTC in circulation, with no unauthorized Bitcoin transfers or token minting detected. Aave risk provider LlamaRisk said it would propose restoring the reserve after Threshold’s operations return to normal.

#5019. Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase has received final approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to charter Coinbase National Trust Company, the crypto exchange said on Monday.

The OCC approved the application last Friday, according to Coinbase. The firm said the charter will let it expand its custody business, which holds digital assets on behalf of institutional clients, under a federal regulatory framework.

"Custody is foundational to our business," the company said, describing itself as the leading crypto custodian globally. The charter will "bring clear and consistent federal uniformity" to its custody operations, it added.

"Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain," Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong tweeted on Monday, calling the trust company "another step for building that foundation."

Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain.

Coinbase National Trust Company is another step for building that foundation. https://t.co/35K2yC3xpj

— Brian Armstrong (@brian_armstrong) November 9, 2026

Coinbase has offered regulated custody since 2018, when its Coinbase Custody Trust Company won a limited purpose trust charter from the New York Department of Financial Services. The new national trust company builds on that foundation while bringing its trust activities into a consistent federal framework, Coinbase said.

The approval comes seven months after the OCC gave Coinbase conditional approval in April, when the company said it was not becoming a commercial bank and would not take retail deposits or engage in fractional reserve banking. Coinbase first applied for the charter last year.

Coinbase joins a growing list of crypto firms with national trust charters. Circle won final approval in July for a trust bank to manage the reserves behind its USDC stablecoin, and Ripple received final approval last month for a trust bank behind its RLUSD stablecoin.

Others are still awaiting final approval. Jack Dorsey's Block has applied for a charter to custody Bitcoin in September, Kraken parent Payward filed its own application in May, and a trust company linked to Trump family-backed World Liberty Financial won conditional approval in August for a charter tied to its USD1 stablecoin, Reuters reported.

The approvals have drawn opposition. Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) has argued that the OCC's crypto charter approvals, including Coinbase's, are illegal, and the Independent Community Bankers of America has sued the OCC in an effort to stop crypto firms from entering banking through what it describes as a side door.

#5018. Korean police book 80 Tesla drivers over illegal FSD unlock devices

Seoul police have booked 80 people for using aftermarket devices to switch on Tesla’s “Full Self-Driving” in cars that aren’t allowed to run it in South Korea. Officers also seized 21 of the devices.

The Seoul Metropolitan Police Agency announced the case on Tuesday. It’s the largest enforcement action yet against the FSD “jailbreak” hardware that has spread through China, Europe, and Korea over the past year.

Police say the suspects either imported the unlock modules from Europe and China or made their own. One plugged a European-made unit into his own Tesla. Another, an IT worker, built his device with information he found on GitHub.

Suspects also swapped advice online on how to unlock FSD and how to stop the car from keeping any record that it had been activated.

They’re charged under Korea’s Motor Vehicle Management Act, which bans unauthorized software changes that can affect a vehicle’s safety. The penalty is up to two years in prison or a fine of up to 20 million won (about $15,000).

The Ministry of Land, Infrastructure and Transport asked police to look into it back in April after finding signs of unauthorized activation. A police official told Korea JoongAng Daily that “safety risks were confirmed,” including the possibility of FSD misreading driving conditions. The investigation is ongoing, with police consulting the ministry and the Korea Transportation Safety Authority on the technical side.

Tesla launched FSD (Supervised) in South Korea in late 2025, but only on US-built vehicles: Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck. Those cars are exempt from Korean safety certification under the Korea-US free trade agreement. The Model 3 and Model Y, which Tesla ships to Korea from Giga Shanghai, haven’t cleared Korea’s safety standards for the feature.

And those two models are the bulk of the fleet. Citing Carisyou Data Lab, Korea JoongAng Daily reports that 47,458 of the 229,311 Teslas on Korean roads as of August, or 20.7%, can legally use FSD.

That number doesn’t match the government’s own count from a few months earlier. In May, data released by lawmaker Park Yong-gap showed just 4,292 of 180,684 registered Teslas (2.4%) were eligible: 2,708 Model X, 1,193 Model S, and 391 Cybertruck. Either way, the vast majority of Korean Tesla owners can’t legally use FSD, and some of them paid over $6,000 for it anyway.

The hardware is the same, which is why the hack works. The modules plug into the car’s CAN bus and get around the regional software lock.

Tesla has been fighting the devices itself. In April, it started remotely detecting them and stripping FSD from affected cars, warned owners they’d be fully liable for any crash and could lose warranty coverage, and permanently banned some Chinese owners from FSD.

Tesla & Starlink: The reality as reported in 2021: every one of our Tesla autos are connected 24/7/365 to the company. It’s easy to see why people do this. Picture a Korean owner of a Shanghai-built Model Y who paid for FSD, watching the Model X next door run the same software on the same computer. That’s frustrating.

Tesla sold FSD to owners of cars it had no regulatory path to enable in Korea, the same way it sold “Full Self-Driving” in Europe for years while approval kept slipping. It only dropped the name there this month after Germany pushed back. Sell a feature people can’t legally use and you create the market for the jailbreak.

Korea has now shown it will actually prosecute. So will Tesla refund the Korean owners who bought a feature their cars may never be allowed to run?

#5017. Zcash devs propose another block time cut days after NU7 upgrade

The proposed reduction aims to shorten transaction confirmation times and improve the user experience for point-of-sale payments, exchange deposits and cross-chain swaps.

Zcash developers have proposed reducing the blockchain’s target block interval from 25 seconds to 20 seconds, seeking faster transaction confirmations days after activating the NU7 upgrade.

On Sunday, developers ValarDragon and Evan Forbes submitted a draft Zcash Improvement Proposal (ZIP) on the Zcash community forum, saying that they want the change to be included in the next community poll and considered for the upcoming NU7.1 network upgrade.

The reduction would shorten the expected wait for initial transaction confirmations. According to the proposal, this would improve the user experience for point-of-sale payments, exchange deposits and cross-chain swaps.

The developers said the change would not increase the network’s overall daily capacity for private transactions. The proposal would also preserve Zcash’s existing issuance schedule, despite blocks being produced more frequently.

The proposal comes just days after Zcash’s NU7 upgrade went live. On Friday, the NU7 upgrade was activated on the mainnet, cutting the network’s target block interval from 75 seconds to 25 seconds.

The upgrade also introduced a Network Sustainability Mechanism designed to recycle a portion of transaction fees into future mining rewards while preserving Zcash’s 21 million ZEC supply cap.

#5016. Sykeruns X7 electric dirt bike review: A lot of power for surprisingly little money

The mini electric dirt bike market has exploded over the last few years, with seemingly countless new brands offering everything from pint-sized electric pit bikes to surprisingly powerful off-road machines. The Sykeruns X7 falls somewhere in the middle, offering enough power to get into some serious trouble while keeping the price relatively approachable.

I’ve spent some time now riding the X7, and while it certainly isn’t the most refined electric dirt bike I’ve tested, there’s enough to like about what you’re getting for the money.

In fact, if you’re looking for a reasonably powerful electric dirt bike for recreational trail riding without spending several thousand dollars, this might be worth a closer look.

The Sykeruns X7 is a compact electric dirt bike featuring a 5,500W peak-rated motor, which translates to around 7.4 horsepower. That’s quite a bit of power for a bike at this price point of $1,699, especially considering that many similarly priced electric two-wheelers are significantly less powerful. Oh, and for our readers, we’ve gotten you set up with a 20% discount. Just use code ELECTREK20 to drop the price down to an impressive $1,359!

Sykeruns claims a top speed of 57 mph (92 km/h), though since this is an off-road only bike, you’ll rarely get the chance to put that claim to the test. I certainly didn’t, as I didn’t have a long enough and flat enough stretch of trail where I felt comfortable hitting nearly highway speeds on a low-cost e-moto.

The battery is a 60V 27Ah pack, providing 1,620 Wh of capacity. Sykeruns claims up to 60 miles (96 km) of range, though as with virtually every electric dirt bike, your actual range will depend heavily on how aggressively you’re riding. Fast trail riding, steep climbs, and repeated hard acceleration can eat through battery capacity much more quickly than leisurely cruising. Suffice it to say that you’ll likely not see the 60 mile figure in real world scenarios, but that’s still a fairly large battery.

The bike rolls on a 17-inch front wheel and 14-inch rear wheel, both wrapped in knobby off-road tires. Hydraulic disc brakes handle stopping duties, while an inverted suspension fork and rear monoshock help smooth out the bumps.

At 143 lb (65 kg), the X7 isn’t exactly featherweight, but it’s also far from being a full-size electric motorcycle.

The first thing I was surprised about with the X7 is that it offers a pretty decent amount of performance for the money. There’s enough power here to have some real fun, and the bike doesn’t feel like one of those underpowered mini electric motorcycles that struggles once you get beyond casual neighborhood speeds.

That said, I wouldn’t describe the acceleration as mind-blowing, either. We’re not talking about the kind of explosive power delivery you’d get from a high-end electric dirt bike costing several times as much. Instead, the X7 delivers a healthy amount of acceleration that feels appropriate for a recreational off-road machine.

And let’s be real here, that’s probably what most people shopping in this budget category are looking for.

The suspension is another area where the X7 earns a passing grade without necessarily winning any awards. The front fork and rear shock do their jobs, absorbing the bumps and ruts you’d expect to encounter on typical trails. It’s nothing I’d call plush, but I could see it doing light jumps with minimal air time.

Would I want to take this thing to a motocross track and start launching it off massive jumps? Not particularly. The suspension doesn’t have the premium feel or sophisticated damping of more expensive machines.

But for general trail riding, exploring dirt roads, and the occasional overlanding shenanigans, it’s perfectly serviceable. Not every electric dirt bike needs to be a competition-ready machine, and the X7 seems comfortable occupying that more recreational category.

I was also pleasantly surprised by the tires, which seem well suited to the bike’s intended purpose. The knobby tread offers a reasonable amount of grip for off-road riding, and I didn’t find myself immediately wishing for a tire upgrade.

The overall size is worth mentioning, too. This isn’t a particularly large electric dirt bike, but at my modest 5’7” (170 cm) height, I felt comfortable on it. The riding position didn’t feel overly cramped, and the bike was manageable enough that I didn’t feel like I was wrestling with an oversized machine.

Taller riders might find it a bit more compact than they’d prefer, but for average-height riders, the proportions seem reasonable.

If there’s one area where the X7 reminds you of its budget-friendly $1,699 price tag, it’s the overall level of refinement.

Nothing about the bike struck me as particularly premium. The components, finishes, and general construction are more in line with what I’d expect from a relatively inexpensive electric dirt bike.

But that’s not necessarily a criticism. There’s a difference between something feeling inexpensive and something feeling inadequate, and the X7 generally stays on the acceptable side of that line.

The controls are straightforward, the digital display provides the basic information you need, and the overall package feels focused on delivering usable performance rather than impressing riders with fancy features.

The grippy hydraulic brakes are also a welcome inclusion, especially considering the speeds this bike is capable of reaching. With a machine that can approach highway speeds, having proper motorcycle-style braking hardware is important.

Of course, long-term durability is a separate question. Budget electric dirt bikes don’t always have the same track record, parts availability, or established dealer networks as the more recognizable motorcycle brands. That’s something worth considering before purchasing, particularly if you plan to ride hard or frequently.

One thing I appreciate about the Sykeruns X7 is that the company makes its intended use fairly clear.

There’s a large red warning sticker on the bike explaining that it’s intended for off-road use only. That’s a good thing, especially in today’s market, where the lines between electric bicycles, electric dirt bikes, and electric motorcycles are becoming increasingly blurred, despite laws existing to keep these types of bikes out of the bike lane and off of public roads.

The X7 is absolutely not an electric bicycle. It doesn’t have pedals, it produces several thousand watts of power, and it’s capable of speeds far beyond anything permitted under electric bicycle regulations.

It’s an off-road electric motorcycle, plain and simple.

Interestingly, though, Sykeruns has equipped the bike with features like a tail light and turn signals, which could potentially give some riders the wrong impression.

Those are certainly useful features on a motorcycle, but they don’t magically make a vehicle street legal. Without the necessary certification, registration, and other road-use requirements, the X7 remains an off-road-only machine.

I worry that some buyers might see those lights and assume they can ride the bike around town like a small electric motorcycle. That’s not the case, and it’s important to understand that distinction before buying one.

After spending time with the Sykeruns X7, my biggest takeaway is that this is a reasonably capable electric dirt bike at a pretty compelling price.

It’s not the best electric dirt bike I’ve ever ridden. The suspension is fairly basic, the build quality is unremarkable, and there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about the design.

But for $1,699 (or $1,359 if you use the code ELECTREK20 to take 20% off!), you’re getting a 5.5 kW peak-rated electric motorcycle with a sizable battery, proper off-road tires, hydraulic brakes, and enough performance to have some genuine fun on the trails.

I wouldn’t say that the X7 here is trying to compete with more up-market electric dirt bikes from the likes of Juiced, Sur-Ron or Talaria on overall refinement, but they make this type of riding much more accessible.

And that’s really where I think the X7 makes the most sense.

If you’re an experienced dirt bike rider looking for a highly refined machine with excellent suspension and premium components, you’ll probably want to keep shopping.

But if you’re someone who wants a fun electric dirt bike for exploring private property, cruising around trails where motorized riding is permitted, or just enjoying some recreational off-road riding without spending a fortune, the Sykeruns X7 offers a pretty reasonable package.

It doesn’t need to be amazing at everything. At this price, being pretty good at most things is already an accomplishment.

#5014. Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

Space and Time, a Microsoft-backed blockchain data project, has launched a platform designed to let businesses use private data in AI tools without handing over the underlying records, the company announced on Monday.

Space and Time Enterprise lets companies prove that calculations made on their private data are correct, so that AI applications, auditors and other stakeholders can check the results without accessing the database itself. Launch partners include onchain asset manager Maple Finance, Indonesian vehicle maker and distributor Indomobil, Oracle software consultancy Trenez and AI data network The Data Foundation.

"Businesses want AI agents to execute workflows using that information, but those agents don't need unrestricted access to the records behind every calculation," Nate Holiday, CEO of MakeInfinite Labs, the original developer of Space and Time, told Decrypt.

The stakes are high, he argued. "The potential cost to a business is the overall value of the business," Holiday said. "Giving AI tools unrestricted access to data creates another avenue of exposure."

Holiday said the company is working with customers in industries including construction, engineering and manufacturing that want to rebuild their systems around AI, so that agents, reporting tools and compliance teams can work from the same business records and check the calculations they rely on.

The platform is built on Proof of SQL, Space and Time's patented technology for proving that database queries were computed correctly. It already works with Google Cloud's BigQuery, and businesses can now run it on their own servers without operating a blockchain, buying specialized hardware or relying on an outside proving service, according to the company.

With GPU acceleration, supported queries over millions of rows can be run and proven in under a second, Holiday said, though actual performance depends on the query, hardware and dataset.

The technology proves that a calculation was carried out correctly on a given set of records, but it cannot tell whether those records were accurate to begin with. "An incorrect entry still needs to be caught through the business's data controls," Holiday said, "but verification gives those controls a much stronger foundation: evidence of which data was used and whether it was processed correctly."

Space and Time raised $20 million in 2022 in a strategic funding round led by M12, Microsoft's venture fund. Last year, Grayscale launched a trust giving investors exposure to SXT, the token that powers Space and Time's blockchain. Earlier this year, the company launched Dreamspace, an AI-powered tool built with Microsoft's Azure AI services that lets users create and deploy onchain apps without writing code.

Space and Time also runs SXT Chain, a decentralized network that supplies verifiable data to DeFi, tokenized assets and stablecoins. Asked whether enterprise customers would create demand for SXT, Holiday said businesses could deploy the product in their own environment, and that the network would come into play as their applications "need to connect to onchain markets," with those workloads paid for through Space and Time.

The company's cryptography is also used elsewhere. Microsoft's Nova project includes Blitzar, Space and Time's cryptographic acceleration library, and the team's work on a commitment scheme called Dory contributed to a16z crypto's Jolt proof system, Holiday said.

#5013. Bitcoin ETFs shed $1.3B in biggest weekly outflow since late June

Investors pulled a net $1.21 billion from BlackRock’s IBIT last week as Bitcoin fell below $76,000 and US technology stocks sold off.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) recorded $1.28 billion in net outflows last week, their largest weekly withdrawals since late June.

The withdrawals brought the funds’ net outflows for the year to about $919 million, according to SoSoValue. The funds had already posted $515 million in net outflows in October.

Bitcoin fell below $76,000 last week as traders digested the fallout from Binance’s $6.3 billion hack, uncertainty after the US midterm elections and a sell-off in technology stocks.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT), the world’s largest spot Bitcoin ETF by assets, recorded $1.21 billion in net outflows last week, according to Farside Investors data.

The withdrawals extended IBIT’s losing streak to six consecutive trading sessions, with approximately $1.44 billion pulled from the fund since Oct. 30. IBIT accounted for about 95% of the weekly total reported by SoSoValue.

IBIT recorded net outflows for six consecutive trading sessions through Friday, bringing withdrawals since Oct. 30 to about $1.44 billion.

Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) drew about $93 million in net inflows for the week, despite losing nearly $200 million Thursday.

Shifting expectations for US interest rates, uncertainty after the midterms and weakness in technology stocks weighed on crypto markets, Bitfire Research said in a statement shared with Cointelegraph.

Bitfire said investors were also reassessing whether Congress could pass the CLARITY Act before year-end.

Related: Bitcoin price dips under $75K as crypto markets miss tech-stock rebound

Bitfire cited a Crypto Fear & Greed Index reading of 25 and a negative Coinbase Premium, which indicates Bitcoin was trading at a discount on Coinbase relative to the indicator’s benchmark.

The Crypto Fear & Greed Index. Source: Alternative.me

Bitfire described the market as being in a “breakdown and bottom-seeking phase,” pointing to upcoming US inflation data and signals from the Federal Reserve as factors to watch. It said that a sustained break below the $74,400–$75,000 support range could lead to further selling.

Bitcoin traded at $74,049 at publication, below the lower end of that range. It was down 5% over seven days and 2.3% over 24 hours, according to CoinGecko.

#5011. Strategy Buys $82M in Bitcoin, Buys Back $188M of STRC

Strategy Buys $82M in Bitcoin, Buys Back $188M of STRC

Strategy bought 1,036 BTC for $81.9 million last week, its second straight weekly purchase, the company said on Monday.

The Bitcoin treasury firm paid an average of $79,012 per coin between November 2 and November 8, according to an SEC filing. It now holds 850,364 BTC, acquired for a total of $64.16 billion at an average of about $75,456 per coin.

Strategy has acquired 1,036 $BTC and repurchased $188M of $STRC. As of 11/8/26, we hold 850,364 BTC and $4.9B of USD Assets. $MSTRhttps://t.co/dNzTPPjday

The purchase is larger than the week before, when Strategy resumed buying with 811 BTC for $65.5 million after a one-week pause.

Strategy also bought back 1,887,464 shares of STRC, its variable-rate preferred stock, for $188.3 million, down from $208.2 million the previous week. It funded both the Bitcoin and the buyback by selling 2,051,348 shares of MSTR stock for $283.8 million in net proceeds, with the remaining $13.6 million going to its USD Cash account.

That marks a change from last week, when Strategy paid for its STRC buyback from USD Cash, drawing the balance down to $132.7 million. With the new proceeds, USD Cash rose to $146.2 million as of November 8.

Strategy's separate USD Reserve, which covers dividends on its preferred stock and interest on its debt, stood at $4.77 billion after it paid out $21.1 million in preferred dividends during the week. About $560.3 million remains available under its preferred stock repurchase program, and $1 billion under its program for buying back MSTR stock.

The update comes two days before index provider MSCI is due to publish the list of companies affected by its new rules on firms dominated by a single asset, which could remove Strategy from its equity indexes.

Strategy's preferred stock is also adjusting to a new payment schedule. Shareholders approved daily dividends on its four preferred stocks last month, starting with STRC on November 1.

#5010. Binance, Tether and Trump crypto firms face Senate records deadline

Sen. Richard Blumenthal asked four crypto firms to start sending records by Nov. 30 and warned of further action if they miss the Jan. 4 deadline.

US Senator Richard Blumenthal gave Binance, Tether and two Trump-linked crypto firms until Jan. 4, 2027, to voluntarily turn over records sought in Senate inquiries.

Failure to meet the January deadline could prompt further action, he warned.

Blumenthal, the ranking Democrat and incoming chair of the Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, sent follow-up letters Thursday to Binance, Tether, World Liberty Financial and Fight Fight Fight LLC, the company that launched President Donald Trump’s TRUMP memecoin.

The letters asked the companies to begin turning over records by Nov. 30. The Jan. 4 deadline falls a day after Democrats are due to take control of the Senate.

Blumenthal wrote that the subcommittee would consider “alternative measures to obtain compliance” if the companies miss the January deadline. The panel’s current rules allow its chair to issue subpoenas for documents and records.

Cointelegraph sought comment from Binance, Tether, World Liberty Financial and Fight Fight Fight LLC but did not receive responses before publication.

Related: US senator probes Tether after reported deal with ex-Trump crypto adviser

In May 2025, Blumenthal opened an inquiry into potential conflicts of interest involving Trump’s crypto ventures. He sought records from both firms on their ownership, foreign investments and safeguards against conflicts of interest. World Liberty Financial’s lawyers disputed his claims and said the company had no affiliation with Fight Fight Fight LLC.

In June 2026, Blumenthal and four other Senate Democrats called for hearings into a reported $500 million deal for an Abu Dhabi investment firm to buy a 49% stake in World Liberty Financial.

In February, Blumenthal asked Binance for records on transactions linked to Iranian entities and Russia’s shadow fleet. Binance called the underlying reports false in its March reply and said that, to its knowledge, no Binance account had transacted directly with an Iran-based entity. In Thursday’s letter, Blumenthal said Binance still hadn’t turned over any of the requested records.

Blumenthal’s letter to Tether renewed requests for records on the use of USDT by sanctioned entities and on Tether’s dealings with Nxum, a consulting firm co-founded by former White House crypto adviser Bo Hines.

In September, Blumenthal released a report by the panel’s Democratic staff alleging that USDT has become a key part of Iran’s shadow banking network. Tether disputed claims that USDT provides a haven for sanctioned actors, saying it helped authorities freeze nearly $550 million in Iran-linked tokens in 2026.
