Thompson), I’ve spent time in just about every contiguous U.S. state. Some, as you might imagine, are more interesting than others—and Indiana isn’t exactly topping the list. Specifically, the fragrance is inspired by City Methodist Church in Gary, Indiana.

Built in the 1920s, the church is still admired for its grand Gothic architecture. Over the years, its roof caved in, but the stone walls remained intact, allowing vegetation to grow throughout its halls. Equal parts creepy and beautiful, it’s exactly the type of place I’d like to sneak into to see if I could spend a night without freaking out.

It’s sophisticated without being pretentious, and noticeable without being loud or overpowering. After applying two to three sprays to the neck and one to my wrists, the cologne maintains most of its strength for seven to eight hours, but lingers much longer. My girlfriend has noticed whiffs of it on my skin as much as 12 hours after applying it.

There is also, of course, a Gothic backdrop to the scent. I tend to get sad at the beginning of November, as it’s no longer socially acceptable to talk extensively about horror movies throughout the day now that Halloween is over. #4978. Google Takes Aim at Nvidia With New AI Chips Built for Privacy and Massive Memory

The TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max join Google’s eighth-generation Tensor Processing Unit lineup, giving businesses four configurations for training and running AI models. Google says it serves nearly 100 TPU customers, from AI labs to universities.

Unlike Nvidia’s widely used graphics processing units, TPUs are purpose-built AI accelerators. Google developed them for its own services but increasingly sells access to outside companies, including on-premises customers.

“With computing moving from human to agent scale, businesses are asking for accelerators that can reason across AI models’ massive context windows,” wrote Amin Vahdat, Google’s chief technologist and senior vice president of AI and infrastructure, in Google's announcement.

TPU 8tx targets a problem that many serious businesses have when it comes to adopting AI: protecting sensitive information while a model is using it.

Data is encrypted in storage and during transmission, but processing typically requires decrypting it in memory. Google says the configuration uses hardware-level confidential computing to isolate customer data and proprietary model parameters even during operation.

The system combines TPU 8t silicon with fifth-generation AMD EPYC processors and Google’s low-latency Boardfly network. Google claims it can cut latency by up to half versus TPU 8t, with almost no performance penalty for the added protection.

Companies could fine-tune and run models on the same system instead of transferring sensitive information between clusters. However, confidential computing cannot prevent a poorly designed AI agent from mishandling data it is allowed to access.

TPU 8i Max addresses another limitation: memory. Autonomous agents analyzing data, codebases, documents or hours of video, etc; need fast access to far more information than conventional AI tasks.

Google combines two TPU 8i chips into one package, doubling computing power, on-chip SRAM and high-bandwidth memory throughput versus TPU 8i. The design packs twice as much silicon into the same rack footprint.

“Our fourth system, TPU 8i Max, is built for a different problem: the memory wall,” Vahdat wrote.

Google is open-sourcing TPU Sync, software that moves AI working memory between chips, servers and storage. It claims the system can reduce certain data-transfer delays and response startup times by more than 90%.

Google's announcement comes as Nvidia faces mounting pressure on Wall Street. Shares plunged 9.3% on Thursday and fell another 5% in Monday's trading after CEO Jensen Huang admitted to an extramarital affair with a woman suspected by U.S. intelligence of spying for China. Nvidia's board has launched an independent investigation, although the company says it has no evidence that corporate secrets were compromised.

The scandal adds to concerns about Nvidia's growth as competitors target its AI chip dominance. OpenAI is reportedly planning to purchase Google's TPUs to reduce its reliance on Nvidia. This is still unconfirmed, but Google's expanding chip lineup could give AI developers more bargaining power, threatening Nvidia's market share and future growth expectations.

Google is competing on specialized hardware and software, not just chip speed. Its open-source TorchTPU project also lets developers run PyTorch and Hugging Face models on TPUs without rewriting their applications.

Still, Google’s performance figures compare its new systems with earlier Google hardware, not Nvidia’s latest products. The company did not disclose pricing or availability dates for the two new configurations.

#4976. Tesla Semi catches fire on I-880 with no crash, closes freeway for 10 hours

A Tesla Semi caught fire on Interstate 880 in Hayward, California, on Tuesday evening, and there was no crash. The electric truck’s lithium-ion battery burned long enough to keep the southbound freeway closed for about 10 hours.

The driver got out unhurt. But a Class 8 electric truck catching fire on its own, in traffic, is something Tesla and its growing list of Semi customers need to explain.

The fire broke out at around 5:35 p.m. PT in the southbound lanes near Route 92. CHP Hayward then closed I-880 in both directions, citing “the potential risk of hazardous chemical smoke,” and posted these photos from the scene:

🚨 TRAFFIC ALERT – I-880 FULL CLOSURE 🚨A Tesla semi-truck has caught fire on I-880, resulting in a complete shutdown of both northbound and southbound lanes due to the potential risk of hazardous chemical smoke. pic.twitter.com/O01z32nxpZ

The driver told officers the truck caught fire and that they didn’t know why. No other vehicles were involved.

Northbound reopened Tuesday night. Southbound took much longer. CHP initially warned that the battery could burn for 15 to 18 hours, but crews beat that estimate, and CHP Hayward posted that all lanes were open at around 3:30 a.m. Wednesday.

The CHP photos show firefighters laying foam around the burned-out tractor, with the front of a Premier Trailer Leasing trailer charred behind it. The truck’s operator hasn’t been identified, and Tesla, which doesn’t have a press office, hasn’t commented. The cause is under investigation.

We’ve been here before. In August 2024, a Tesla-operated Semi caught fire on I-80 near Emigrant Gap while driving from Livermore to Tesla’s facility in Sparks, Nevada. At the time, no crash was reported. The NTSB later found the truck had left the road on a curve, hit a delineator post and a tree, and its battery ignited after the impact. Firefighters used about 50,000 gallons of water, and the lanes stayed closed for up to 15 hours.

Hayward is different. Without a crash, the focus shifts to the truck itself: the battery pack, the high-voltage system, the drive units, or something as ordinary as a tire or brake fire that spread.

And the timing isn’t great for Tesla. The company only started volume production of the Semi in Nevada this year, after nearly a decade of delays. WattEV ordered 370 Semis in May, mostly for drayage at the Port of Oakland, just a few miles up I-880 from where Tuesday’s truck burned. In September, Tesla was also named lead supplier on a 2,500-truck ZET SCALE order.

First, some perspective, because this fire is going to get used as an anti-EV talking point. Truck fires are common. According to NFPA data, US fire departments responded to an average of 15,462 large-truck fires per year between 2018 and 2022. That’s about 42 a day, in a fleet that’s overwhelmingly diesel. Mechanical failure was a factor in 44% of them, and only about 2% were tied to a collision.

The same goes for cars. NFPA and DOT data put the US average at one vehicle fire for every 17 million miles traveled, and combustion vehicles make up the vast majority of that fleet. Tesla claims one fire per 140 million miles for its own vehicles. That’s Tesla’s number, but independent data, like government figures we’ve covered before, points the same way: gas vehicles catch fire more often than EVs.

This is not the main reason that pretty much all the other trucks have moved to LFP, but it is still one more reason that looks like a good idea.

Also, designing your own cells is higher risk... at this point, can they even switch supplier?

But that doesn’t get the Semi off the hook. Diesel fires happen across millions of trucks. Tesla doesn’t disclose Semi deliveries, but PepsiCo, its launch customer, was up to about 86 trucks in 2024, and volume production only started a few months ago. With a fleet that small, one fire with no crash is a big data point. It’s also the second Semi fire to shut down a California interstate in about two years.

So we need the cause. If a tire, a brake, or the trailer caught fire and it spread to the tractor, that’s a regular trucking problem. If the pack went into thermal runaway on its own, Tesla needs to find out why before tens of thousands of these trucks hit the road, and fire departments need a better plan than letting a loaded truck burn on a freeway for half the night.

The NTSB investigated the last one. It should look at this one too, and Tesla should publish what it finds. Will it?

Electric trucks and cars are only as clean as the electricity charging them. If you’re driving an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. #4975. The $2.85B Binance hackers lost shows why the UK needs stablecoin freeze rules

The Binance hack proved stablecoin freezes work. Now the UK should make clear freeze rules a condition of listing coins.

Opinion by Lissele Pratt, co-founder, Capitalixe

Last weekend, the Federal Bureau of Investigation claimed that North Korea was behind the $6.3 billion Binance hack, which analysts are calling the largest heist on record.

While I’ve read a lot about the scale of this heist, far less has been said about what went right.

Because while it is true that the perpetrators stole $6.3 billion, they haven’t actually been able to hold on to a significant proportion of those takings. $2.85 billion of the funds has been frozen.

The reason for this comes down to what the stolen money was comprised of. Of that $2.85 billion, 94% was held in stablecoins.

While this hack was terrible news all around, it did show that centralized control, oftentimes criticized by crypto purists, might actually be one of the strongest tools we have against crypto crime.

Because, unlike other assets, stablecoins can be frozen by their issuers, giving victims a real chance to stop stolen money reaching the wrong hands.

It does, however, raise an uncomfortable question. If private companies have the power to freeze billions of dollars in assets, who decides when they should be allowed to use it?

The United Kingdom now has the opportunity to do just that.

To get an idea of just how much the freeze mattered, you just have to look at what the hackers did within the first hour of their heist.

Related: Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

Clearly, they understood that there was a very limited window to move the stablecoins before anyone could stop them. So that’s exactly what they did.

What was frozen from the Binance hack. Source: Elliptic

Less than an hour after the hack began, they had already sold $325 million worth of stolen USDT (Tether’s stablecoin) for Ether. The reasoning behind this was to swap an asset that could be frozen with one that could not.

Luckily, stablecoin issuers were quick to act, with Tether’s first major freeze coming just 37 minutes after the stolen funds reached the hackers’ main wallet, and Circle following three minutes later, blocking the movement of USDC.

Without Tether and Circle intervening, far more of the funds could have ended up beyond their reach.

The freeze, to its credit, worked for the most part because a handful of companies decided, in real time, to use it.

The problem is that there still isn’t an agreed standard that outlines when a freeze is justified or how quickly the response needs to be, and we’re beginning to see the consequences of not having these answers.

For instance, Circle is facing a proposed class action from Drift Protocol investors who allege it failed to freeze around $230 million in stolen USDC after the April hack. Their argument is that Circle could have stopped the money from moving, but didn’t act in time.

Meanwhile, Tether is being sued in New York by two businessmen who claim it froze millions of their assets months before a seizure warrant existed. It’s important to mention that Tether has responded to this case, calling these allegations baseless.

Working in payments and banking, it’s not uncommon for me to see traditional banks freeze money and accounts. Due to this, you may argue that if a bank can do this frequently, why shouldn’t a stablecoin issuer be able to?

Stablecoin freeze timeline. Source: CertiK

My argument isn’t that they shouldn’t. But banks don’t get to decide they want to freeze assets out of nowhere. There’s usually a legal trigger behind their decision, as well as a way to challenge it.

Stablecoin issuers now hold the same power while having to act faster and across borders. So it’s about time they’re given some structure.

There is, however, a caveat with regulating stablecoin issuers. Tether and Circle, two of the largest issuers, aren’t based in the UK, so British regulators can’t simply tell them what to do.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

What Britain can control, though, is which stablecoins UK customers are allowed to buy, and they’ve already begun doing so.

In June, the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) published its cryptoassets regime. Under it, UK trading platforms must carry out due diligence and publish a disclosure document before they list a coin, and that includes stablecoins issued overseas. It comes into force on 25 October 2027.

The Bank of England is finalizing its Code of Practice for systemic stablecoin issuers by the end of this year. It covers stablecoins used widely enough for payments to matter to UK financial stability.

Neither framework establishes the clear obligations around freezing stolen funds that I believe are necessary. But both are still taking shape, giving the UK the perfect opportunity to address this gap now, rather than trying to retrofit new requirements later.

So here’s a solution that I believe would work. Before a stablecoin can be sold on a UK platform, its issuer should have to agree to two conditions.

The first being that it is contactable and ready to act. Tether and Circle did exactly that last week, but the victim was the world’s biggest exchange. A small UK platform should get the same response.

I work with a number of smaller crypto businesses that don’t have Binance’s profile or a direct line to Tether. Under the current system, they’re the ones most likely to be left waiting.

The second is that there needs to be a clear timetable for what happens next. If an issuer freezes funds, it should notify the National Crime Agency that same day. The freeze should then expire if there hasn’t been a court order put out to keep the wallet frozen.

Neither of these are extremely controversial takes. They’d give law enforcement a chance to intervene when money is stolen and also protect people from having their assets frozen indefinitely, without legal grounds.

The Binance hack has shown us that stablecoins can give victims of crypto crime a chance to stop stolen money before it’s gone for good. That shouldn’t be a privilege reserved only for the world’s biggest exchanges.

#4974. James Spader Has Never Been Afraid to Get Horny on Main

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The 66-year-old has spent four decades mastering the art of playing many different styles of pervert. VisionQuest proves he’s still got it.

IT CAN BE hard to keep track of the constantly shifting spaces of politics and culture, so you can be forgiven if you’ve missed arguably the biggest development in either space over the past few months, playing out as we speak: James Spader is back. The actor, who has spent four decades specializing in playing many different styles of pervert, has returned as yet another one in Disney+’s VisionQuest, the third and final chapter in a trilogy that also included WandaVision and Agatha All Along, and a work that might also be one of the first real considerations we’ve seen of a dystopian post-human world navigated exclusively by artificial intelligence agents. The show may not be for everyone, but Spader, as has often been the case since the ’80s, is doing truly unbelievable work.

Spader plays the physical manifestation of Ultron—the proto-AI doombot he voiced in 2015’s Avengers: The Age of Ultron, who has now assumed the form of James Spader, sporting a feral salt-and-pepper beard, hexagonal specs, and defense against the dark arts professor loungewear. He is glorious, bloviating on subjects from cyberpunk philosophy to fatherhood in a scotch and cigar baritone with a hint of mischief dancing behind his eyes.

With each passing VisionQuest episode, Spader not only helps push the show’s knotty plotting forward, but chews on each bit of scenery with the best of them (he’s particularly effective when sharing the screen with T'Nia Miller, who plays another high-minded scientist-turned-synthetic being). Spader’s history as the magnetic degenerate who captivated Hollywood in the ’80s and ’90s is more than evident in each episode of Marvel’s latest series, especially if you know where to look for it.

Spader, now 66, started his career as a calculating piece of shit in Pretty in Pink, before hitting it big on the American indie film scene, starting in Steven Soderbergh’s smash hit Sex, Lies & Videotape as, what else? An unnervingly direct, impotent drifter who interviews women on camera about their sexual experiences then beats off to the footage. From there he had brushes with conventional stardom in kind of blockbusters like Stargate, or, trying to belie his lot in life, miscast as straight men to Rob Lowe or John Cusack, but through the ’90s he becomes the maestro of the psychosexual thriller, showing up as obsessive compulsive rapist werewolves, hitchhiking gamblers, hooker-banging lawyers, and widowers who can’t get their face out of Susan Sarandon’s crotch. When you work on projects like these—writing about the entirety of a successful working actor’s filmography—there’s often a staggering level of diversity between genres and roles. What’s staggering about Spader’s career is how little of anything else there is. It’s all delinquent sicko shit.

It is poetic that for the most part, at the turn of the century, Spader migrates to television right around the time the sex thriller goes out of style. He ages, and wins long overdue acting trophies, on the small screen. Perhaps most interestingly, Spader now may be as interesting a presence as he ever has been. Through the years, he’s gained weight and lost hair and his voice has deepened, but he continues to ooze libertine, alpha sexuality and idiosyncratic intensity.

With his return to Marvel as a face, as well as a voice, the time was right to clarify, in the parlance of basketball clip packages on YouTube, “James Spader was a problem,” in several senses of the word. People today might not realize there was a time a Hollywood star could be defined by playing, and presenting to the public as a seductive, creepy, sex pest weirdo, and there was a steady diet of projects that gave them opportunities to do that specific thing on screen. In Spader’s work specifically, we see a moment in American life and culture when Hollywood still had the guts, and the temerity, to be horny on main.

Spader has referred to himself as “Brat Pack Adjacent,” emerging with that cohort of young stars but never quite “of” them. His primary moment comes in this John Hughes classic as an asshole who wears a blazer to school with the sleeves cuffed. He might be the gold standard of a quintessential sniveling ‘80s high school bully this side of William Zabka.

It makes sense that near the beginning, then at the outset of the late period of his career, Spader faced off against Robert Downey Jr., because in a certain light Spader can be seen as Downey’s slightly misshapen clone. Both are twitchy, motor-mouthed wise-asses incapable of a straight line delivery, who contain deep and limitless reserves of quippy charisma, only Spader’s juice is slightly tainted. Downey is right on the line of smarmy and seductive, and Spader is just over it. He’s alluring but a little off-putting, a little annoying, a little frightening, and always compelling. In Less Than Zero you worry for Downey, lost in Bret Easton Ellis’s debauched Los Angeles Neverland. You don’t worry about Spader, who is right where he wants to be, operating with impunity and ruling comfortably in hell.

Spader’s first mature role is less “Spadery” than basically anything he'd done before or would do afterward, thanks in large part to the way he weaponizes his soft-spoken openness. He’s the only character in the film who is honest about who he is and what he wants in a seedy drama where everyone lies or elides, talking around their desires.

Young Roland Emmerich was aiming for a dumbass version of an ‘80s Spielberg space adventure, so for his version of Richard Dreyfuss, he chose shaggy, adorable, baby James Spader in an era when oddballs like Nic Cage could become action stars. Spader went in a different direction with his career, but we’ll always have this performance as a linguist/allergy-prone dweeb with a shoulder-length shag presenting ancient aliens as an academic argument, using hieroglyphics to unlock an artifact liquid portal that transports you across the universe. He’s perfectly paired with Kurt Russell, as two different phenotypes of asshole.

No two movies have led to more hilarious mix-ups than this David Cronenberg-directed pervert classic, adapted from a J.G. Ballard novel about people with a desire for sex wrapped in a desire for self-immolation, and Paul Haggis’s shallow, sappy, Oscar-winning exploration of race relations of the exact same title. There’s too much to say about this film to confine it to a blurb, but I’ll just mention that a mere 25 minutes into the film, Spader is having sex with America’s Sweetheart, Holly Hunter. At this point, she is the widow of a man who died in a car accident with Spader. They are in a car identical to the one that killed her husband, one that Spader has just purchased and nearly wrecked again, with both Spader and Hunter in the car. One of the most depraved films I’ve ever seen. Watch it immediately.

Before moving to TV, Spader had a wild turn of the century: He’s in a lot of duds that don’t really know what to do with him. In a way, it exposes what a unique and specific tool he is, making his long, prolific career even harder to wrap your head around—because we see how easily it can go wrong for him. One is a bizarre ghost rom-com with Michael Caine and Maggie Smith, another is a near unwatchable post-Seven thriller with an egregiously bad Keanu Reeves as the antagonist killer, and the last, an abhorrent sex comedy. It’s interesting to watch Spader fail, trying to fight against his nature and remake himself into something closer to a conventional leading man. Luckily, he found a medium that better understood how to channel his dark peculiarities.

Another fetish movie, this one based on a Mary Gaitskill short story. Or, as I like to think of it, “Straight Pillion.” Spader is perfectly measured here as a stammering, mean, tender dom that uses his OCD in a film about control and submission.

After a test run on The Practice, this is the beginning of James Spader: TV star, the dawn of the persona that will become his final form. The show may appear to be a conventional primetime legal drama, and sometimes it is, but it possesses deranged and horny mid-aughts sexual politics that would cause riots if it came on air today. This is because it feels tailored to Spader (and co-star William Shatner) and his various kinks and proclivities.

“There is no such thing as a product, don’t ever think there is. There is only sex. Everything is sex. You understand that what I’m telling you is a universal truth?” This is how much of a generation of Americans first meets Spader, as the enigmatic boss daddy Robert California, on an NBC sitcom that was averaging nearly seven million viewers per episode at the time. When Steve Carell exited The Office, Ed Helms stepped into his role in Dunder Mifflin’s office as well as the sitcom’s dynamic as goofy office manager. But Spader also came along, injecting something entirely different. As California, he’s a literal confidence man whose tieless collar is unbuttoned, his receding hair is tousled, and he holds a dark hypnotic power over the office—one not even John Krasinski’s cool-headed Jim is immune to. Throughout the show’s eighth season, the entire office competes for the favor of Spader’s expert power-dynamic manipulator, a soft reprisal of his role in Secretary.

After spending weeks delving into this strange, delightful filmography, it pains me to think there are many people out there of a certain age who will mainly think of Spader as “The Blacklist guy,” but that’s what 218 episodes and 10 years in a role will do to your public perception. Despite frequent notices it was filming in my neighborhood for many years, I never caught this show on air. Having screened a few for consideration, I now see it as both a step down, and a fulfillment of Spader’s whole career journey. On one hand, he’s something of a diet Hannibal Lecter, a mastermind criminal who turns himself over to the Feds to help take down criminals worse than he is (for reasons that aren’t initially clear). Over time, the show exposes its conventional, maudlin heart through made-for-TV beats. Spader is fully washed at this late date in life, and should be commended for not fighting that. Still, he’s always dominant, remaining a few steps ahead of his friends and foes, a high T individual with a working cock, using it frequently.

In other words, he remains a glorious deviant—to the bitter end.

#4973. AI Data Centers Are Buying Used Jet Engines as New Power Turbines Take Six Years

AI Data Centers Are Buying Used Jet Engines as New Power Turbines Take Six Years

AI data-center developers are hunting for used gas turbines and retired jet engines because ordering brand-new power equipment could leave their servers waiting for electricity until the next decade.

Some companies are buying refurbished machines, while others are converting aircraft-engine technology into generators that can run alongside their computing facilities.

New gas turbines now take an average of six years to deliver in the United States, according to energy consultancy Wood Mackenzie. Delivery discussions are already focused on 2030 and 2031, a timeline that clashes with an AI industry trying to bring enormous computing campuses online much sooner.

Global turbine orders have topped 100 gigawatts, compared with manufacturers' estimated annual capacity of just 60 to 70 gigawatts, Reuters reported. Siemens Energy, GE Vernova, and Mitsubishi Heavy Industries have all said orders are outpacing what they can produce.

Gas turbines can generate large amounts of electricity by burning natural gas to spin equipment connected to a generator. AI data centers need that power around the clock to run densely packed chips and cooling systems, and connecting new facilities to public electricity grids can itself take years.

The International Energy Agency expects global data-center electricity consumption to nearly double from 485 terawatt-hours in 2025 to 950 in 2030. Consumption at AI-focused facilities is projected to triple over the same period.

One way around the backlog is an aeroderivative turbine, a power generator built using technology adapted from aircraft jet engines.

Other companies are going further by seeking older machines and retired aircraft engines that can be rebuilt for stationary power production. For example, AI infrastructure firm Crusoe ordered 13 units from Missouri-based ProEnergy in April, each rated at 50 megawatts, for a combined 650 megawatts. The jet-engine-derived equipment is intended for future U.S. data centers, but the contract doesn’t mean that Crusoe bought engines removed from retired aircraft.

Second-hand machines can also carry a substantial discount. Used turbines in good condition sell for about $1.5 million to $1.7 million per megawatt, compared with roughly $2 million to $2.5 million for new units, according to equipment brokers and energy analysts interviewed by Reuters.

One broker described a sale of two smaller turbines totaling 29 megawatts for $12.5 million. Equivalent new equipment would have cost about $35 million and taken five years to arrive, he said.

“That secondary market is very, very hot right now,” EthosEnergy CEO Ana Amicarella told Reuters, adding that asking prices are climbing as more buyers compete for the same machinery.

Building a power plant beside a data center also brings fuel needs, air emissions, and permits into the equation. The Environmental Protection Agency says stationary turbines and engines used by data centers are subject to federal air-pollution standards.

Last week, OpenAI promised to cover electricity and grid costs at future campuses it owns and operates after voters backed tighter restrictions on data-center construction.

Power-market research firm Cleanview identified about 90 gigawatts of proposed on-site generation at U.S. data centers in its mid-2026 analysis. Only about 2 gigawatts was operating at the time

Developers are looking beyond turbines, too. Finnish manufacturer Wärtsilä told Reuters it had booked around 3 gigawatts of orders from U.S. data centers since spring 2025 for generators powered by gas engines rather than turbines.

#4972. New retro electric motorcycle to debut at EICMA with a novel feature: a second gear

Most electric motorcycles have something in common: a single-speed transmission. But a British company is preparing to shake things up at this year’s EICMA motorcycle show in Milan, where it will unveil a new electric motorcycle demonstrator featuring an unusual two-speed transmission designed to shift gears without interrupting power delivery.

The company behind the technology, E-Motiv Shift Systems, has developed what it calls a seamless powershift transmission, promising improved acceleration, better efficiency, and a more engaging riding experience.

Unlike conventional motorcycle transmissions, which generally require a brief interruption in power during gear changes, E-Motiv’s system is designed to maintain torque throughout the shift.

That could offer some interesting advantages for electric motorcycles, where a lower first gear could provide stronger acceleration while a taller second gear could help optimize motor efficiency at higher speeds.

Of course, most electric motorcycles don’t actually need multiple gears, and very few have ever offered multi-speed transmissions. Electric motors produce strong torque across a wide operating range, which is why manufacturers generally prefer the simplicity, lower weight, and reduced maintenance of single-speed drivetrains.

But that doesn’t mean a second gear couldn’t offer some benefits.

E-Motiv’s technology actually traces its roots back nearly 25 years to the Zeroshift transmission system, which eventually found its way into Aprilia’s MotoGP racing motorcycles in 2015.

It's possible, from my perspective, that running a motor at lower rpm under reduced load like flat terrain cruising, could improve range, and lower temperatures, and might be beneficial for cars and trucks as well.

The latest electric version was developed through a £1 million UK government-funded project involving motorcycle manufacturer BSA, the University of Bath, and the Institute of Advanced Automotive Propulsion Systems (IAAPS).

The demonstrator motorcycle, known as Demo 1, was conceived by British design consultancy Envisage and is being built by IAAPS. Real-world testing is scheduled to begin in early 2027.

E-Motiv will showcase the technology at EICMA from November 3-8, though the demonstrator remains a development project rather than a production-ready motorcycle.

Whether two-speed transmissions eventually become common on electric motorcycles remains to be seen. But if E-Motiv can deliver meaningful efficiency and performance improvements without adding too much weight, cost, or complexity, it could give future electric motorcycles another interesting trick up their sleeves.

#4970. Is Your Penis Getting Smaller as You Age? This Common Factor Could Hide One to Two Inches.

IT PROBABLY SOUNDS like a nightmare scenario to think your penis is getting shorter. Maybe not even being visible at all. But it can happen as you age, and when it does, doctors refer to it as a buried penis.

Now it's not that your penis is physically getting shorter. It's just that most of it is hidden. More on that below.

CONVINCED YOU LOST an inch? Thankfully, your penis isn’t disappearing; it’s just hidden beneath fat tissue.

Justin Dubin, MD, urologist and men’s health specialist at Baptist Health, says that generally when you can’t see the penis, it’s usually because of low abdominal tissue surrounding the penis.

Essentially, your penis is still its regular size, but it doesn’t extend outside the body. Drogo Montague, MD, a urologist in Ohio tells MH what that looks like. “There is an opening where the penis would normally hang, and the head is usually flush with skin, but there’s no shaft outside of the body.”

Now you don’t just wake up one day with inches of your penis vanished. The effect occurs on a continuum, says Dr. Montague. You might notice at first, for instance, that your penis seems to get shorter and shorter. (Though, this is not the same as the small amount of shrinkage that some men see with age, which is due to skin cells around the penis becoming less elastic.)

“This is a serious condition because it will inhibit urination, intimacy, and personal hygiene,” says Scott Miller, MD, urologist and medical director of Wellstar Urology. “It can also strongly impact self-esteem and overall emotional health.”

MOST CASES OF buried penis are due to one thing: obesity.

While the rates of buried penises haven’t been thoroughly studied, Dr. Montague sees more men in his office today dealing with the issue than he has in the past. His booked-up calendar is no coincidence. The World Health Organization reported that 1 in 7 people is living with obesity. And even though we are living in the golden age of GLP-1s, half of adults worldwide are expected to be overweight or obese by 2050.

The penis itself has two erection chambers. About one-third is inside the body and two-thirds are outside in the penile shaft. When you’re overweight or with severe obesity—and noticing a change in length—what’s changing is basically how much of your penis is inside your body and how much is outside.

“With extreme obesity, it’s like the obese body engulfs the penis. A full buried penis is when the entire penis shaft is buried below the surface of the skin,” says Dr. Montague. So it's still there, but you just can't see it all.

There are a few factors, some starting at birth.

There is a low chance of a baby being born with abnormalities in which the ligaments that attach to the penis are weak. Fortunately, Dr. Dubin says this is usually identified and surgically corrected early on.

Another cause can be “any surgery on your penis” that’s gone wrong, says Dr. Dubin. That includes circumcision, a procedure done on 71.2 percent of American guys are circumcised at birth (or even as an adult). If a doctor does not remove an appropriate amount of foreskin, there is a risk of the penis dropping back into the fatty area surrounding it.

But any procedure that involves the penis runs the risk of scar tissue and possible infection. That includes penile injections and penis enlargement.

“The ironic part is that while a lot of these things try to make your penis look bigger or be bigger, the complication is it can make your penis actually smaller and cause buried penis,” says Dr. Dubin.

Lymphedema can cause swelling in the scrotum. As lymph fluid accumulates, you might see the makings of a buried penis.

Also known as acne inversa, the skin condition can create small and extremely painful lumps under the skin. Lumps usually appear in the armpits, inner thighs, and—you guessed it—your groin. This happens because the body mistakenly attacks hair follicles in the sweat glands, causing pockets of pus and other scarring.

Sometimes when the prostate is removed due to prostate cancer surgery, men may lose about an inch of penile length. “That’s the result of pulling the urethra up to attach to the bladder,” says Dr. Montague. Still, it seems like most penile shortening rebounds as time passes after your surgery, so don't let that stop you from getting your prostate checked.

The condition occurs when scar tissue forms in the penis and it causes a curved erection. The curvature may give the appearance of a shorter length. The issue often arises because of injury to the penis during sex or micro-tears that accumulated from sports or physical accidents affecting the genital area. Fortunately, if diagnosed early, there are drugs and procedures that could straighten out the penis. Read more about it here.

WHEN YOU HAVE a buried penis, certain activities become more difficult. Right away, you might find it hard to pee. Dr. Dubin says guys may not be able to get the stream started and might have poor flow. Buried penis can also cause trouble in the form of dribbling and incontinence.

The area is also tough to clean, which leads to a host of issues that might trigger fungal or bacterial infections or skin breakdown. Buried penis is associated, for example, with a rare skin condition called lichen sclerosus. It’s an inflammatory disorder that causes itchy patches. The skin that hosts these patches becomes very delicate.

Beyond that, penetrative sex remains an ongoing issue. “These men can’t have sex or sex is very difficult,” says Dr. Montague.

Mentally, having a buried penis can cause depression and self-esteem issues. “Most men associate their penis with their masculinity, and they associate fertility with masculinity,” says Dr. Dubin. “These kinds of issues can have strong mental health implications.”

IF YOUR BURIED penis is obesity-related, losing weight will definitely help. But keep in mind that, even after weight loss, you might have excess skin and tissue that keeps your penis buried.

“Sometimes, unfortunately, in very obese people, the damage has already been done, and more aggressive treatment options may be required,” Dr. Dubin. In that scenario, you may need surgery—which is also an option for people who don’t lose weight. Dr. Miller says surgeons would focus on removing the surrounding fat and tissue and repositioning the penis.

“That allows the penis to come outside of the body,” says Dr. Montague. “In most buried penis surgeries, skin and fat are cut out.”

Having a buried penis can be embarrassing to talk about, Dr. Dubin says. It's disturbing to have this happen and it can take a toll on your well-being. If you feel it's getting in the way of living fully, consider talking with a counselor or other mental health professional. It's also crucial to see a doctor if you’re having urinary, hygiene, or sexual health issues because of a buried penis.

#4969. Analysts see no broader ‘contagion’ from $6.3B Binance hack

Binance’s pledge to cover all user losses helped contain the fallout from the $6.3 billion hack, analysts said.

Binance’s pledge to cover user losses from its $6.3 billion hack helped contain the fallout across crypto markets, analysts told Cointelegraph.

“There doesn’t seem to be any contagion-type effect from the hack, given that Binance is able to cover the losses using its balance sheet,” Martin Lee, market insights lead at DWF Labs, told Cointelegraph. He said investors turned their focus to the implications of the US midterm elections.

Binance’s daily net outflows fell to $458 million in the 24 hours leading to Monday’s publication time, down from $6.1 billion on Nov. 2 after withdrawals resumed, according to DefiLlama.

Attackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance hot wallets in the Oct. 29 breach, making it the largest crypto hack to date. Bitcoin (BTC) fell as much as 5.7% over the first five days after the hack, a decline CryptoQuant called “modest” in its Wednesday report .

Bitcoin fell roughly 13.6% after the $1.4 billion Bybit hack in 2025 and about 9.9% after the Coincheck hack in 2018. At least 14 of the past 38 hacks CryptoQuant reviewed were followed by steeper Bitcoin declines.

Bitcoin’s drawdown after the Binance hack, compared to previous exchange hacks. Source: CryptoQuant

Major exchange hacks have previously triggered “short-lived drawdowns that markets later absorbed” as long-term holder demand returned, Lacie Zhang, research lead at Bitget Wallet, told Cointelegraph, adding that the Binance hack does not represent a “structural trend reversal” for crypto markets.

Related: Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

“Much of the hack’s impact now looks priced in,” with Bitcoin currently trading at an “attractive level,” Bitfinex analysts told Cointelegraph.

“Confirmation of a market bottom now depends on the Consumer Price Index (CPI) reading due on 10 November, which will shape the Federal Reserve’s next rate decision. Higher bond yields would put pressure on risk assets.”

Market participants priced in an 80% chance that the Federal Reserve will raise rates at its Dec. 9 meeting, up from 69% a month earlier, according to the CMEGroup’s FedWatch tool.

Bitcoin fell 2.5% over 24 hours to $74,580 as of 1:21 pm UTC on Monday, according to CoinMarketCap.

#4968. Ford casts 5,000 ‘unicastings’ for Fathom EV truck in Tesla-style shift

Ford has now produced more than 5,000 large aluminum “unicastings” for the Fathom, its upcoming $28,350 midsize electric pickup, as it fine-tunes the process ahead of a 2027 launch.

Each truck gets two of them, front and rear, replacing 146 individual parts. It’s the clearest sign yet that Ford is all-in on the manufacturing approach Tesla popularized with its Giga Press six years ago.

The update comes from Vladimir Bogachuk, Ford’s chief engineer of advanced vehicle structure architecture, in a post on Ford’s From the Road blog. The castings are made at Ford’s Shelbyville Technical Center and shipped to Louisville Assembly Plant for process testing and prototype builds. Louisville is the Kentucky factory Ford is retooling for the Fathom and other vehicles on its Universal EV Platform.

Ford melts aluminum ingots at about 1,300°F (700°C) and injects the metal into either a front or rear structure mold. Once a robot pulls the part out, it goes into a quench tank, gets trimmed and machined, and then runs a gauntlet of checks: industrial X-ray scans for pores and voids, dimensional measurements in a climate-controlled room, optical surface scans, and lab tensile and chemistry tests on samples.

Nothing ships to Louisville until a batch clears every inspection. Ford says it’s also working on cutting cycle time and on designing the castings for “modular repairability.”

That last part has been the main reason why automakers have been reticent about larger cast parts. Repair cost is the biggest knock on giant castings, since a damaged one-piece structure can turn a moderate crash into a write-off. Lucid executive Cory Steuben has said insurance concerns drove the company’s decision to skip gigacasting altogether.

Tesla kicked this off in 2020 with the first IDRA Giga Press at Fremont, a machine with roughly 6,000 tons of clamping force. It cast the Model Y’s rear underbody in a single piece, replacing 70 parts, and Elon Musk said the change removed the need for about 300 robots.

Tesla wanted to go much further. It added a front casting, tied the two together with a structural battery pack, and moved to even bigger presses for the Cybertruck. But in 2024, Reuters reported that Tesla had backed away from its plan to cast nearly the entire underbody in one piece, and the automaker has reportedly since dropped the front casting on some Model Y production.

So Ford’s 146-to-2 figure looks great on paper. But it’s a front-and-rear layout, basically the same architecture Tesla launched with on the Model Y. Ford isn’t leapfrogging Tesla here. It’s catching up, just with a vehicle that will cost less than any Tesla on sale.

Since that first Giga Press, a growing list of automakers have bought huge presses or committed to them:

Anyone have real world experience with how much the cost of repairability with Tesla Gigacasting? As mentioned in article likely to get totaled by insurance and/or expensive to fix compared to the traditional modular approach, shifting cost to consumer to reduce corporate costs.

Others aren’t buying it. Stellantis has said it won’t invest in gigacasting, while BMW and Mercedes-Benz have stuck with smaller presses for body parts. Hyundai pushed its “Hypercasting” plan back two years.

We’ve been tracking this casting race since Tesla’s first Giga Press showed up in Fremont, and the lesson so far is simple. Fewer parts is great, but the press alone doesn’t make a car cheap. The whole factory does.

That’s why Ford’s approach is worth watching. The unicastings are one piece of a bigger overhaul at Louisville, where Ford is splitting the line into three branches (front, rear, and the battery pack with the interior floor) that meet at the end. Ford says that can be up to 40% faster than its current assembly, or about 15% net once it reinvests some of the savings into automation and insourcing. It’s very close to the logic behind Tesla’s “unboxed” process, and they are betting it’s what actually gets you to a $28,350 truck.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4966. Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.0 Are Impressive, But Be Prepared to Sell a Kidney to Afford Them

Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.0 Are Impressive, But Be Prepared to Sell a Kidney to Afford Them

Anthropic released Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2 on Monday, promising AI models that can turn a handful of instructions into working software, design physical objects, and tackle research problems that previously required teams of specialists.

You can now ask a chatbot to build the app you've always wanted. You may also need to sell a kidney to pay for it.

That's not because every prompt costs thousands of dollars. It's because Fable can keep working for days, writing code, testing it, and fixing its own mistakes while the meter keeps running.

Some users have already tested its capabilities, and they’ve been impressed—by the power, and the price.

AI YouTuber Matthew Berman put the model through a series of ambitious tests and described Fable 5.2 as the “best AI model” he's ever used. His projects included playable video games and a browser-based version of Microsoft Excel, complete with formulas and pivot tables.

One single level of Gears of War: E-Day-inspired level took 43 hours and 48 minutes of build time and processed 8.63 billion tokens—small chunks of information an AI reads and produces. The running API cost estimate, based on pay-as-you-go pricing for developers, had reached $14,710.

I've burned over 100 billion tokens early testing Fable 5.2. It is BY FAR the best model I've ever used.

Here's my full review (including Gears of War clone):

> Fable 5.2 is the most autonomous model to date. Given a task with little direction, it makes… pic.twitter.com/0xTzqTY040

— Matthew Berman (@MatthewBerman) November 9, 2026

The Excel clone ran up an estimated $11,700 after discounts for cached inputs, or information the system can reuse instead of processing from scratch. Berman also showed a Fall Guys-style game that consumed more than 9 billion tokens over 71 hours, equivalent to roughly $13,000.

Those are estimated API costs, and the projects weren't flawless: Berman reported awkward animations, bugs, and features that still needed testing.

If you’re poor, well… Fable also knows how to work for cheap. For less than $40 (two months of Claude Pro) it built a cute orange cylinder.

Another experiment simulated water-balloon physics for $91. It’s worth noting this isn’t a model intended for routine tasks. This is for serious work and people with deep pockets.

In its launch announcement, Anthropic said the new models generate output up to 30% faster than their predecessors. The company also says Fable helped solve two open theoretical-physics problems involving black holes and design three functioning electronic devices, claims that will need independent scrutiny.

Anthropic's internal FrontierCode 2.0 evaluation, which measures the percentage of proposed coding changes that would be accepted into real open-source projects, put Fable 5.2 at 49.1%, compared with 32.4% for Fable 5.1. That helps explain why users are pushing it beyond simple chat responses.

Fable and Mythos share the same underlying model, but Fable has stricter safeguards around dangerous biological and cybersecurity requests. Mythos is reserved for verified organizations working in those fields.

The standard API price is $8 per million input tokens and $40 per million output tokens, down from the previous generation's $10 and $50. Optional fast mode can generate output up to 2.5 times faster, but doubles the rates to $16 and $80.

For contrast, Kimi 3.1 Pro, a model considered by many as the best Chinese AI model costs $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens. The latest Deepseek model costs $0.6 and $1.98 outside of peak hours.

Per Anthropic’s standards, Fable’s price is still twice the per-token price of Claude Opus 5.5, which Anthropic recommends for everyday work. Its flagship is meant for problems the cheaper models struggle to finish, not for asking whether your email sounds too aggressive.

Just last week, speculation swirled around a supposed Fable 5.5. The actual release is 5.2, with cheaper token rates but enough appetite to turn a hobby project into a serious expense.

Anthropic, last we checked, doesn’t accept kidneys as a method of payment.

#4965. Alan Cumming Can’t Be Tamed

At 61, the Traitors host has made a career of playing by his own rules. From leading his dark new series Tip Toe to reprising his X-Men role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, he's always eager to take on a different form.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

“I’M GEARING UP towards hacking it all off,” Alan Cumming says as he twists a finger around an errant silver tendril of hair dangling across his forehead. “I usually like to end a job and do something—get it cut off or get it colored. Something.”

The 61-year-old Tony-winning-Broadway-star-turned-Emmy-winning-reality-TV-host is the consummate chameleon. In just the past year, he’s played the “dandy Scottish laird” host of The Traitors: New Blood, reprised his role as the X-Men’s resident lizard-like teleporting mutant Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, and took on one of his most emotionally and physically challenging roles as gay bar owner Leo in Tip Toe.

The impulse to change—both on screen and off—is therapeutic. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, I think, as a reaction to my childhood trauma about my father's obsession with my hair,” he says. Cumming’s memoir, Not My Father’s Son, opens with a scene of his father taking a rusty pair of sheep shears to his temple in a fit of rage. “They were blunt and dirty and they cut my skin, but my father shaved my head with them, holding me down like an animal,” Cumming writes in the first chapter.

The trauma followed him into early adulthood, and he experienced visceral anxiety whenever he got his hair cut. Eventually, however, the act of transformation transformed Cumming. “Hair was always something I felt I had no control over as a child,” he says. “So I wanted to have as much control over it and change it as much as possible, almost in a slightly perverse way.”

Of course, the choice to change isn’t always his, and sometimes the choice is taken away from him. It was “painful,” he says, to be unable to change his hair when he was working on certain projects, like his seven seasons on The Good Wife. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, but I don't often have my own hair,” Cumming adds. “It's usually for some job.”

Today, however, his natural silver hair seems purposefully unstyled as his fingers coax through it. That is, when he isn’t using his hands to assume the statuesque pose of his Traitors persona or demonstrate the boxing stance from his Marvel superhero training. Simply talking about a performance becomes a performance, his eyes bulging behind a pair of thick-rimmed black glasses and his arms gesturing widely underneath his faded red T-shirt as he describes the immense physicality required of these recent roles.

The walls of his Scottish home are white and surprisingly sparse. You’d have no idea that the person living there is now, by his own admission, best known for “swanning around” a Scottish castle “like an insane little prince” in increasingly outrageous outfits (so outrageous, in fact, that Traitors contestants often applaud when he enters the room after a wardrobe change). Or perhaps the walls are white so he stands out all the more. Either way, the Scottish charm is working overtime on a Sunday evening.

“I think both with my hair and, more lately with what I wear, I do sort of feel like a little dolly that people dress up,” he says before adding, with a good-natured laugh, “I don't mind it.”

In a brief moment between transformations, Cumming sat down with Men’s Health to discuss his ever-changing style on Traitors, how he trained for Avengers: Doomsday, and what he considers to be the ultimate fountain of youth.

MEN’S HEALTH: You often talk about your fashion choices with each Traitors season, but what goes into choosing your hairstyle?

ALAN CUMMING: There was a feeling that because we were doing the civilian version, we could reboot and bring things down a little. So, we decided just to go a little more simple and classic with the hair. But it was hilarious, because every morning I would walk downstairs and say, ‘This is toning it down. What the fuck?’ I guess I wasn't dressed as a coffin or a bride. The plan was to go back to the dandy Scottish laird a little, but it still was pretty mental. But that's why I didn't have lots of color and things in my hair. But we're about to start another Traitors season in a couple of weeks, so all bets are off again.

MH: You're in your 60s now, and you still have wonderful hair. Did you ever have a fear of going bald when you were younger, or would that have maybe been freeing in a way?

AC: I've never feared going bald. I don't think I would care. It's easy when you’re not the bald person saying this, but I don't quite think going bald has the same power it used to. I feel like so many people shave their heads nowadays anyway. And there are loads of actors I know who are bald and wear wigs all the time. When you're an actor, it's obviously easier—and actually probably better—to be bald, because you get more hairdo options.

MH: Outside of Traitors, you're also reprising your role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday. How was doing your own stunts in your 60s?

AC: I did some stunt work, but I didn't do all my own stunts. Please don't think that. There are lots of things I'm not allowed to tell you about. I got into trouble for giving the game away earlier on. But I mean, I fight some people. There's loads of fighting in it. If you enjoy fighting and you enjoy superheroes, this movie's for you.

I loved the training. They always train you for much more than you actually need, but I did it. I was boxing with these French boys. And I’d just made a film in France, so they were very impressed with my French and the fact that I could box a little and not have a heart attack.

It’s really funny when people, especially younger people, are so impressed by your fitness. I'm quite bendy as well, so people are always impressed by my flexibility. And I guess I am 61, but, I mean, I'm not dead. I'm not a thousand years old. I've luckily not had any sort of physical things going wrong. I don't really feel any different, to be honest, but it’s also hilarious when young people are just amazed that you've been able to stand up and not die.

MH: How long were you doing this boxing training for?

AC: I only did a week or so with those boys. But I used to take boxing lessons in New York, and I really want to get back into it again. It's such good exercise, especially before you actually get up and spar—all the core stuff and strength stuff—and it's such fun as well.

My boxing teacher was called Mo, and he'd been a big sort of world champion. But one day I went to the gym, and he was really pissed off because he’d seen a music video with some rappers and singers, and they were all playing boxers. He said, "They were just terrible."

And he went, "Drake. Drake was the worst."

And he said to me, "You could take Drake." So now, every time I see Drake, I just think, “I could take you.” Drake better watch out.

MH: Let him know it's on sight. How else did you prepare your body for Avengers?

AC: I actually stay pretty fit all the time. When I'm doing The Traitors, I go to the gym every day, or I do three days gym, two days swim or yoga. There have been times in my life when I've had to get really, really super fit or super skinny or something, so you go nuts. But I like being fit, and I like stretching. I like doing things like that, so I’m pretty constant.

Filming Tip Toe was much more physically demanding, because I knew I was going to have a lot of emotional weight to carry. It was a really disturbing show. So, I would exercise a lot, but I would also get two Thai massages on the weekend because my body was so tense.

If you do a film like X-Men and you're jumping around, yes, sometimes your body can get hurt in that way, but emotionally it wasn't really very demanding, whereas Tip Toe was so intense. Physically, I'm so stressed a lot of the time, and David Morrissey, the other actor, is shoving me around. So, my body was all screwed up. What's really important in those situations is to plan ahead and to make space for you to give your body some chance to recover.

MH: How do you think about your body differently as you take on each new role?

AC: I'm a very physical actor. I think about how the person physically behaves and how they move and stand and… [laughs]. I'm laughing because my husband is just over there, and he always watches me in things and says, "Oh, you look so different. You just disappeared into this character." And then there'll be a scene when I'm running, and he can tell it's me because I run in a weird way. My running always gives me away. But you know who's got the weirdest run ever? Woody Allen. I was once in a film with him and he had to run, and I've never seen anything like it. It just was like, "How is this all happening? How are all these limbs managing to propel someone forward?" It was the craziest thing.

But I'm always very conscious of the physicality of a character. The Traitors is a big example. He's very erect, very sort of grand, quite dancy. He moves like a dancer. And then in Tip Toe, it was really fascinating becoming more cowed as the show went on. He got less confident, so I got more crushed.

With Nightcrawler, he's really fascinating because he's like a little weird lizard or something. So it's a lot of neck stuff. In the first film, I did a lot of work with a movement person to develop the language of that. So, I just remembered all that stuff for Avengers. I suppose it’s muscle memory. After 22 years, I really did remember all that stuff. Also, they gave me a gray wig, which I thought was hilarious. That was a concession to my elder statesman-like thing. I've got gray hair as Nightcrawler now, which is just hilarious. Nothing else has changed, just his hair has grown gray.

MH: You mentioned your longtime husband, Grant. What do you think makes a relationship last?

AC: Having a laugh, being open and kind. Being kind is the biggest thing, but being honest and always trying to talk things through and having a laugh. Also, having time apart.

When I go away to make a film or whatever, we don't see each other for a few weeks. It's actually really great because you miss that person, and you look forward to seeing them and you value the time you do have. It's not just about missing them, but you remember the things that you've always liked, and you're constantly reassessing your relationship because you're looking forward to seeing that person again. Also, it means you maintain your own identity. Obviously, we're a very strong couple, but we have our own lives as well.

So it's a combination of those things, but I really do think you can't underestimate how much being able to laugh is. Also, I think it's good to still have sex. Sex and laughter. I think if either of those go or both of those go, get out, taxi for one. Because they're both the same thing, I suppose—they're sensual pleasures. Those are very important for me, anyway.

MH: Agreed. And so, you mentioned that you're 61 now—

MH: As we all should. But tell me, what's the best part about being 61?

AC: There are lots of nice things. I mean, partly that I forget I'm 61. On the show I'm doing right now, I feel like I can just chat with the kids on the crew who are in their 20s. For me, what's great is being in a position where I'm able to have such strong, long relationships, friendships. With Tip Toe, David Morrissey and I have known each other since the late '80s. Acting against each other in such a horrible way was so great because we were so close and trusted each other. And that was a really good thing about being older—you're so sure of someone and so trust someone. Also, I just love having a life where I do have friends in their 20s and 30s and 40s.

I equate oldness with lack of curiosity. You can be very old and still be very curious, but whenever anyone says, “Oh, I'm too old for this,” I think it's like a death knell somewhere. A little fairy dies when you say that.

I enjoy not giving so much of a fuck. I'm really happy in my life and things don't bother me as much as they used to. I think it'll all come out in the wash. We'll work it out. I feel I'm much more sanguine about things, especially as I've got such a crazy life with so many things going on. It's always this sort of mental schedule and trying to make it all work, but I just now think, “It'll be fine.” So that's a good thing about being older.

MH: What are you most curious about these days?

AC: Lots of things. I'm running a theater now and every day there are things I've got to deal with and talk about and find out about, and that's amazing. I joined TikTok today, so I decided to do that. I want to learn to play the bagpipes. I want to learn to type properly because, I mean, I use my computer, obviously, and I've written books and films, but I've never really properly learned to type. And I want to get better at Spanish, and I have a sort of trad wife fantasy of making gluten-free scones in my AGA, which is a kind of country stove.

AC: I don't really feel this age, but when I was 33, I moved to New York, and I feel like my life hasn't really changed that much since then. I still live in the same area. I still have a lot of the same friends. I do a lot of the same things. I have the same kind of life. I don't go out as much as I used to, but I love dancing still, and I would do that more if people didn't take photos of me all the time. Before mobile phone cameras, you didn't have to worry about being off your tits and dancing in a club with your chums. Now you do.

I'm obviously not 33. I don't look 33. But I feel like I'm as fit as I was when I was 33, kind of.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

#4964. Tesla confirms Vietnam entry to take on VinFast and its 36% market share

Tesla has started hiring in Vietnam, and one of its recruiters says the company is now “officially coming” to the country. The five open roles include a sales manager, a service manager, and a charging project developer.

It’s a tough market to walk into. VinFast, the local all-electric automaker, has sold roughly 36% of every new car in Vietnam this year, more than its next five competitors combined.

Tesla quietly registered Tesla Motors Vietnam LLC in Ho Chi Minh City on September 11, with about 77.7 billion dong (roughly $3 million) in charter capital and a business scope covering car imports, wholesale, and retail. Tesla never commented on it.

Now we have the first confirmation from someone inside the company. Ricky Ip, who recruits for Tesla, posted on X this morning:

Tesla is officially coming to Vietnam – and we're hiring.Our mission is to build a world of amazing abundance and we are looking for passionate people to join us in Vietnam and help make that vision a reality. Current Job Openings:Sales Manager – https://t.co/AvW3higssS… pic.twitter.com/YDZvLNmz8P

The five openings are Sales Manager, Service Manager, HR Business Partner, Purchasing Manager, and Project Developer, Charging. That last one is the tell. You don’t hire someone to develop charging projects unless you plan to build Superchargers in the country.

Tesla has followed this playbook before in Southeast Asia. It went on a hiring spree in Thailand in September 2022 and opened orders there three months later. It started hiring in the Philippines in September 2024 and launched in early 2025. So a Vietnam launch in the next few months wouldn’t be a surprise.

There’s no word yet on pricing, models, or store locations. Oddly, the recruiting graphic features the next-generation Roadster, with fine print saying the vehicle is “for illustration purposes only.” Fair enough.

Tesla isn’t walking into an empty market. It’s walking into VinFast’s backyard.

VinFast delivered 177,697 cars in Vietnam through the first nine months of 2026, out of a national total of about 495,000. That’s about 36% of the entire car market, gas cars included. In September alone, it had 37.7% of the market.

Here’s how the year-to-date numbers stack up against the next brands:

Add up Toyota, Hyundai, Mitsubishi, Ford, and Kia and you get 175,136. VinFast alone beat all five of them.

And it’s already passed its full-year 2025 volume with a quarter left to go. VinFast says September was its 25th straight month as Vietnam’s best-selling car brand.

The model mix tells you a lot about the market. The VinFast VF 3, a tiny two-door electric crossover that launched at under $13,000, is Vietnam’s best-selling vehicle so far this year. VinFast models held the top five spots in September.

VinFast builds its cars in Haiphong. Tesla would have to import everything, most likely from Giga Shanghai, and Vietnam kept passenger cars on its high-tariff list under the ASEAN-China free trade agreement.

Expanding to Vietnam is a logical step considering the other markets nearby that Tesla is already selling in. In these markets, a smaller and affordable car (model 2) would have been a big seller.

Charging is another gap. VinFast’s sister company V-Green runs the country’s dominant charging network and announced plans this year to spend about $380 million on 99 ultra-fast charging stations. Third-party networks in Vietnam are still thin.

There is one thing working in Tesla’s favor. Vietnam just extended its registration fee exemption for battery-electric cars through the end of 2030, and that applies to imported EVs too.

And VinFast’s volume comes with an asterisk. A significant share of its deliveries go to GSM, the Xanh SM electric taxi and ride-hailing company founded by VinFast chairman Pham Nhat Vuong. VinFast has long relied on Vietnam for the bulk of its deliveries, which we pointed out when it delivered nearly 100,000 EVs in 2024.

#4962. Bitcoin, tech stocks brush off Nvidia nerves as analyst mulls $68K target

Bitcoin rebounded from an 11-week low near $73,500 as the Nasdaq Composite rallied despite sell-off in Nvidia shares.

Bitcoin (BTC) returned above $75,000 after Monday’s Wall Street open as tech stocks rebounded.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD up 0.8%, having dipped as low as $73,500 on Sunday, a level not seen since Aug. 21.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The weakness accompanied news that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang may have been the target of a Chinese intelligence effort, a claim Beijing subsequently denied. Nvidia stock fell about 6% to $170.24 on Monday early trading, a level it last traded at on March 31, before rebounding to session highs of $179.92.

Nvidia stock one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In a Sunday note, Dan Ives, senior managing director at merchant bank Yorkville Ives & Co., warned that the episode could create lasting headwinds for Nvidia’s share price.

“This is a black eye moment for Jensen and will spark multiple congressional inquiries at a minimum and will create an overhang on the stock for a myriad of reasons,” he said, according to CNBC.

Gary Gensler, former chair of the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), said that Nvidia’s board would face “tough choices” based on the results of the company’s internal investigation.

Elsewhere, investors sought to buy the lows on tech stocks, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index shaking off the Nvidia claims to gain 2.5% in the first hour of the US trading session.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Falling oil prices helped spur renewed strength in equities and offered Bitcoin some respite from its own downside. US WTI crude fell to $86.19 per barrel on the day, its lowest since Sep. 1, before recovering to around $88.20.

CFDs on WTI crude oil one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In his latest analysis, economist Robin Brooks reiterated gold, Bitcoin and the S&P 500’s negative correlation to oil following the attack on Saudi Arabia’s East-West Pipeline, a key oil-transit route, in September.

“Even if these correlations fade somewhat going forward, it’s reasonable to think that a fall in oil prices will lift these risk assets,” he summarized.

BTC/USD acted in a narrow range between its 21-week and 50-week simple moving averages (SMA) on Monday, with these at $73,078 and $75,492, respectively.

Related: BTC price eyes $73K amid Nvidia volatility: Three things to know in Bitcoin this week

Commenting on current market structure, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, eyed a potential dip toward the 200-week SMA at $67,755, a trend line that last interacted with price in mid-August. At the same time, Alan saw conditions slowly starting to improve on weekly time frames.

“The macro structure and thin order books leave room for lower prices before a breakout can be validated. That test is possible, not inevitable - and it doesn’t have to happen this week. In fact, the weekly view is starting to lean bullish,” he told X followers.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21, 50, 200 SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#4961. CATL pushes ahead with 20 Ah solid-state EV batteries, with 500 Wh/kg in sight

CATL hit another milestone in its solid-state battery development, advancing to 20 Ah cells. The world’s leading battery maker aims to begin small-scale production in 2027.

CATL remains the leading global EV battery manufacturer by a wide margin. According to SNE Research, CATL held a commanding 39.9% share of the global EV battery market through the first half of 2026.

To stay ahead, the company is advancing several new battery chemistries, including solid-state and sodium-ion, promising to be safer, more efficient, and lower cost than traditional lithium-ion batteries.

When investors asked about the progress of its solid-state battery program on Tuesday, CATL said it continues to invest heavily in all-solid-state batteries and expects to begin small-scale production in 2027.

As it has stated before, output is expected to be limited at first as it scales up to mass production around 2030.

Speaking at the Summer Davos Forum in June, CATL chairman and CEO Robin Zeng said that, on a scale of one to nine, all-solid-state batteries have only reached level four.

All-solid-state batteries differ from semi-solid-state batteries in that they require a solid electrolyte to conduct lithium ions, rather than a liquid.

The company uses a sulfide-based solid electrolyte system, which offers high ionic conductivity and deformability, but maintaining stable contact between solid components remains a hurdle.

CATL said its sulfide-based batteries have now entered 20 Ah trial production as it aims to achieve an energy density of 500 Wh/kg.

CATL’s 2027 timeline to begin small-scale production aligns with other automakers and battery manufacturers racing to introduce all-solid-state EV batteries.

Toyota, which has been promising solid-state batteries for nearly a decade now, aims to launch the first all-solid-state battery-powered EV in 2027 or 2028.

Using Sumitomo Metal Mining’s proprietary powder synthesis technology, Toyota claimed to have developed a “highly durable cathode material” for all-solid-state batteries last October. Toyota revealed a prototype last year, claiming it can deliver 745 miles (1,200 km) of range and charge in under 10 minutes.

However, like CATL, Toyota expects production to be limited and will not reach the mass market until closer to the end of the decade.

Samsung SDI, on the other hand, aims to begin mass production of its SolidStack all-solid-state batteries in the second half of 2027.

BYD, which ranks second in global EV battery sales behind CATL, also plans to launch its first solid-state battery-powered vehicle in 2027.

In an exclusive interview with Carwow.es last month, executive vice president Stella Li said BYD is in “the leading position” on solid-state batteries and will “prove that” next year.

BYD’s battery division, FinDreams, aims to begin producing sulfide-based all-solid-state batteries in limited batches in 2027, ramping up to mass production by 2030.

While solid-state batteries are often hailed the “holy grail” of battery tech, they are only one part of the equation.

It took them about 3 years for pilot to production of LMFP so I believe it. I generally believe CATL and BYD along with many of the others anyway. You can trust the big Chinese suppliers and OEMs on time line and what they have for the most part. Everyone else not a chance. I think Ford figured that out with suppliers.

CATL, BYD, and others continue advancing lithium iron phosphate (LFP) and sodium-ion batteries, which will ultimately create more efficient, lower-cost options that offer various performance needs.

BYD’s second-generation Blade LFP batteries already deliver over 1,000 km (621 miles) of CLTC range. What’s next?

One thing is certain: New battery chemistries and evolving tech will only continue making EVs more efficient, unlocking longer driving ranges, faster charging speeds, and better performance.
