#4956. Tesla’s rocket-shaped Robotaxi map in Houston is partly ‘fake’

Tesla’s new rocket-shaped Robotaxi service area in Houston includes parts of downtown where the service won’t actually pick you up, according to Ethan McKanna, the creator of Robotaxi Tracker and former Tesla employee.

McKanna rode the service after Monday’s expansion and called Houston “by far the most egregious ‘fake’ AV service area” he has seen. One trip quote had him spending about 40 minutes in a Robotaxi to go two blocks.

On Monday, Tesla expanded its Houston Robotaxi geofence into the shape of a rocket. It took the small launch zone in northwest Harris County and stretched it into a long corridor down to downtown and Bellaire, announced with “Houston, we have liftoff.”

McKanna runs Robotaxi Tracker, the crowdsourced site that tracks Tesla’s Robotaxi fleet and service areas, and he worked at Tesla this summer, so he knows the program better than most. He went to Houston to try it out. Here’s what he posted last night:

Another thing. Often AV service area maps advertised are kind of fake- they don’t actually go to everywhere in the polygon the provider publishes. Robotaxi Houston is probably by far the most egregious ‘fake’ AV service area I’ve seenI get they were doing it so they could get… https://t.co/ILXIkmwGVp pic.twitter.com/yUfswZhQKF

The video shows the Robotaxi app with a pickup pin dropped in downtown Houston, well inside the polygon Tesla published. The app refuses it: “Location not available. Move the map to select a different location.”

McKanna acknowledged that AV service area maps are often “kind of fake,” since operators don’t serve every street inside the polygon they advertise. But he singled out Houston as the worst case he’s seen and said he understands Tesla did it “so they could get the starship shape,” which doesn’t seem like a good reason to fake availability.

The day before, he posted a trip quote that shows what this looks like for a rider:

40 min ride to cross 2 blocks downtown.this is pretty rough pic.twitter.com/n8kP4s9evT

The app quoted a 2:05 pm pickup on the east side of downtown and a 2:48 pm drop-off at Houston Police Department headquarters on Travis Street, a couple of blocks away. Instead of cutting across downtown, the planned route runs south through Midtown and the Museum District to the Texas Medical Center area, then loops back north.

McKanna’s read is that the Robotaxi appears to be avoiding railroad crossings downtown, and he said it fits with “other strange routing in Houston.” He expects Tesla to fix it.

To be fair, he wasn’t down on the service overall. He wrote that, apart from that issue, it “feels really good in Houston,” with reasonable wait times and “significantly better driving than my recent Waymo rides.”

Tesla isn’t the only one. On Tuesday, Robotaxi Tracker reported that Waymo’s Orlando service area had shrunk by 10 square miles, down 8.4% to 109 square miles. McKanna explained what happened:

Orlando’s airport was inside the map Waymo published, but McKanna checked the app and it won’t let riders set the airport as a drop-off yet. So the tracker took it out. Waymo’s airport service might be rolling out soon, and someone got ahead of the announcement.

That’s an airport on the edge of the map. In Houston, the dead zone is in the middle of downtown.

The key to understanding Tesla is the stockholders are the real customers, Elon looks at actual paying customers of his products as patsies or marks who will willingly serve as unpaid beta (or alpha) testers and who will be the ones paying or going to jail when something goes wrong.

First, a big thanks to Ethan. Tesla shares very little data about its Robotaxi program, and Robotaxi Tracker has become the best independent source on where Tesla’s fleet actually operates and how big it is. And as Orlando shows, he holds Waymo to the same standard.

Every AV operator has gaps in its map, Waymo included. But Waymo’s Orlando gap is an airport on the edge of the map that it’s likely about to add. Tesla’s Houston gap is downtown, and it drew a rocket first and figured out where the cars could actually go second.

Service and product should always outrank marketing, and that’s clearly not the case with Tesla’s Robotaxi. People rely on those maps to know if Tesla offers the service where they are. Faking the map just to make a rocket shape is completely stupid. Do better.

If you’re thinking about an EV to get around town instead of waiting on a Robotaxi, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4952. Kia’s PV5 electric campervan has two beds and a fully equipped kitchen [Images]

Kia’s electric campervan, based on the PV5, includes two double beds, a 200 W solar panel, and a fully equipped kitchen.

The PV5, Kia’s first 100% electric van, has been a huge success for the Korean automaker. After launching it at the end of last year, the PV5 has already become Europe’s top-selling small (C-segment) electric van.

Based on its modular skateboard platform, called E-GMP.S, Kia can easily swap body styles to build custom configurations.

Although the PV5 is primarily sold in Passenger for everyday driving and Cargo for business use, Kia said the platform unlocks “endless possibilities” for different body styles and conversions, including a full-on electric campervan.

Wellhouse Leisure, a family-run campervan manufacturer based in the UK, revealed the Kia PV5 i-Camper.

The fully electric campervan is “now built and ready to view,” the company said. Based on the PV5 Cargo Plus, the campervan measures 4.69 m long, 1.89 m wide, and 1.99 m tall, which is about the size of a Ford Custom.

Although that’s pretty small for a camper, it offers more than you’d expect. Inside, the PV5 i-Camper includes two double beds with an elevating roof, a full kitchen setup, and a 200 W solar panel.

The sliding seat system enables the two rear seats to fold flat to form a bed and can be removed entirely. The upper bed, under the pop-up roof, has “more comfy” Froli Springs.

In the kitchen, you’ll find a sink with a tap and an electric water pump, two induction burners, a 42 L refrigerator, cabinets and storage spaces, and an optional microwave.

This EV would be a huge hit if imported into the US. Maybe after the huge order backlog has subsided Kia will consider importing the van to the States.

The vehicle also has a lithium leisure battery and a 12V supply, so you don’t need to use the vehicle’s main battery while camping.

It also comes with vehicle-to-load, letting you power external electrical devices, an e-bike, or even a campsite.

The PV5 Plus Long Range model uses a 71.2 kWh battery, providing a WLTP range of up to 247 miles. However, the added equipment will impact the range in the campervan conversion. Charging the battery from 10% to 80% takes around 30 minutes using a DC fast charger.

In the UK, Wellhouse Leisure lists the PV5 electric campervan starting at £63,000, or about $84,000.

#4951. Avalanche’s AVAX leads crypto gains following NYSE tokenization deal

Avalanche’s AVAX token rose as much as 10% after the NYSE selected its blockchain to support settlement on a planned tokenized stock trading platform.

Avalanche’s AVAX token jumped as much as 10% on Monday, making it the top earner among the 100 largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, as the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) announced plans to use its blockchain to settle trades on a new platform for trading tokenized stocks around the clock.

AVAX later eased to $8.86, still up over 8% on the day, according to CoinGecko data as of 03:22 p.m. UTC. The token currently has a market cap of around $3.95 billion, with $439.7 million traded over the past day. The Avalanche blockchain boasts a total value locked (TVL) of $633 million, up 4% over the past 24 hours, according to DeFiLlama.

“At the NYSE, we’re looking at how blockchain technology can expand what we offer in equities, and we’re picking the right technology for each job,” Dennis Lee, the head of digital issuance at NYSE, said in an announcement.

The deal underscores a growing effort by Wall Street firms to use blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and allow investors to trade outside regular market hours. Typically, traditional US stock markets are open from 9:30 am to 4:00 pm EST on weekdays, with trades often taking at least one business day to settle.

The NYSE first announced plans for the platform in January. In September, Ava Labs revealed that the exchange had spent about a year testing Avalanche, but the blockchain’s final pick was not confirmed until Monday.

Related: Aave launches V4 on Avalanche, laying groundwork for tokenized credit markets

Ava Labs said that the NYSE will use Avalanche’s blockchain to keep track of stock trades and help complete transactions on its planned trading platform. The platform will let investors buy and sell digital versions of stocks on different blockchains, while Avalanche will be used to record those trades and make sure transactions are completed.

“The upcoming digital trading platform will support equities listed on a range of blockchains, though the ledger that records and settles trades needs to be fast and reliable, let us control who can take part, keep costs predictable, and have a large community of developers behind it. Avalanche meets all those needs,” Lee said.

The platform could launch before the end of 2026 if it receives regulatory approval, according to The Wall Street Journal.

“Our first conversations with the NYSE team began nearly a year ago, and it’s remarkable how quickly we’ve progressed toward supporting this infrastructure,” John Nahas, chief business officer at Ava Labs told Cointelegraph. “It’s an important step toward blockchain becoming foundational financial infrastructure.”

The news comes as other major financial firms are also investing in blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and available around the clock.

In September, Nasdaq announced plans to invest $100 million in Kraken’s parent company, Payward, to launch tokenized stocks. The expected launch for that project is the second quarter of 2027.

Meanwhile, the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), which helps settle US securities trades, launched a service in October that allows financial firms to turn traditional securities into digital tokens.

“Today marks the culmination of years of work to bring tokenized securities into a regulated framework supported by trusted market infrastructure,” Frank La Salla, CEO of DTCC, said at the time. “By extending DTC’s resiliency, governance and operational standards into digital markets, we are establishing the foundation for responsible institutional adoption at scale.”

The NYSE deal also follows other financial firms’ efforts to bring traditional financial assets specifically onto Avalanche. In July, Japanese financial technology company Progmat announced it had moved more than 452 billion yen worth of digital securities onto a dedicated Avalanche blockchain.

And in September, Aave Labs announced plans to launch a lending service on Avalanche that would let financial firms borrow Tether’s USAT stablecoin using tokenized assets as collateral.

#4948. PNC Bank tests turning USDC payments into dollar deposits

The pilot lets eligible corporate and institutional clients accept USDC on Ethereum, with each token converted to one US dollar before deposit.

PNC Bank, the eighth-largest US bank by assets, launched a limited pilot enabling eligible corporate and institutional clients to receive USDC payments through Coinbase.

The pilot accepts USDC, a US dollar-backed stablecoin issued by Circle, on Ethereum using Coinbase Payments products. Payments are automatically converted into dollars and deposited into designated PNC accounts, allowing businesses to accept stablecoins without holding them.

PNC said the service aims to speed up access to funds and improve cross-border payment efficiency. The bank did not disclose fees, settlement-time benchmarks or the number of participating clients, nor did it respond to Cointelegraph’s request for additional details by publication time.

The pilot builds on a partnership established in July 2025, when PNC agreed to offer cryptocurrency services through Coinbase’s infrastructure while providing banking services to the exchange. The partnership led to the launch of Bitcoin (BTC) trading for eligible PNC Private Bank clients in December.

PNC’s move follows a similar initiative by Citi, which expanded its Coinbase partnership in September to enable institutional clients to accept stablecoin payments through its Spring by Citi platform, with Coinbase converting the payments into fiat currency.

USDC is the second-largest stablecoin by market capitalization, at $70.5 billion, behind Tether’s USDT at $176.8 billion, according to DefiLlama data. The total stablecoin market stood at around $290.8 billion on Monday.

#4943. Steve Buscemi’s Secret to Stealing a Scene

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

“My job is to come in and pretend like it’s my movie!”

I’M LOOKING RIGHT into Steve Buscemi’s eyes, which on this early October morning happen to sit behind a pair of black-framed glasses, stylishly matching the collared shirt and jacket he’s wearing. Looking into Buscemi’s eyes is kinda like looking at the sun—even when he isn’t quite as animated as some of the characters he plays, his demeanor comes across warm and thoughtful. The 68-year-old is a New York native, and it’s easy to picture an alternate world where he’s the neighbor who always greets you in the hallway—instead of a face you’ve seen in a number of classic movies and shows that probably approaches triple digits.

Much like the sun, sometimes Buscemi’s eyes—and the rest of him—are at their strongest when doled out in limited quantities, adding an indescribable excellence to a movie or show in need of some extra oomph.

His latest role comes in Wild Horse Nine, a darkly comedic thriller from writer/director Martin McDonagh, the Oscar-winning Irish filmmaker behind movies like In Bruges and The Banshees of Inisherin. The film follows a pair of longtime friends and CIA officers, Chris (John Malkovich) and Lee (Sam Rockwell), in 1973 as they visit Easter Island in Chile, just ahead of the infamous coup d’état. Chris, who is terminally ill, is essentially being put out to pasture by the agency, and wants to take a visit from their station in Santiago, and Lee joins him. Buscemi plays a supporting role, popping up throughout its runtime as MJ, the spectacularly dressed and morally dubious boss to our lead duo.

Buscemi has played lead roles before, including, most notably, in HBO’s hit series Boardwalk Empire, for which he won a Golden Globe and was nominated for two Emmys. But it’s those supporting roles—in everything from blockbusters like Con Air to classics like Reservoir Dogs and The Big Lebowski to cameos in Adam Sandler movies—that have made him iconic, and it’s those kinds of movies where people see him put some of his best work on display. How, then, does he so regularly make a character like MJ feel lived-in and three-dimensional in a movie that mostly follows Chris and Lee?

“I don’t really worry about what their story is,” he explains with a laugh. “You know? My job is to come in and pretend like it’s my movie!”

He explains that he read Frank Capra’s autobiography, The Name Above the Title, where the It’s a Wonderful Life director makes it clear that he considers every character in one of his films to be worthy of their own standalone narrative. “The thing I care about most is the part I’m playing… it doesn’t matter how big it is,” he says. “But are they important to the story? It doesn’t matter how much screen time he has.”

It’s a busy season for Buscemi, who’s not only in Wild Horse Nine, but also plays a key supporting role alongside his old friend (and fellow Coen brothers collaborator) John Turturro in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York. As the former film hits theaters and with the latter already out, Buscemi spoke to Men’s Health about locking in on his roles, close friendships, and memories with the cast of The Big Lebowski.

MEN’S HEALTH: What draws you to a project?

STEVE BUSCEMI: I mean, I like compelling characters. I like ensemble films and ensemble casts. I like good writing and good directing, and with Martin you get that in one package. Knowing it was a Martin McDonagh script, already I’m saying yes before I even read the script, practically. I’m reading it going, [Laughs] I hope I love this as much as I think I’m going to love it. And I did.

MH: Is there any learning curve when you’re working with someone new, who has such a specific vision of what he wants?

SB: There was a bit of nervousness, only because I wanted to do a good job. I wanted to do my best. I did have the advantage of getting to hang out with him in Venice when Banshees of Inisherin was there, four years ago. And we really got on really well. We laughed a lot together, and so that put me at ease, knowing that he was a gentle soul, and seemed like he was going to be very open and collaborative. But I was still just nervous because, you know, the material is just so high up there, and knowing it was going to be John, and Sam… you never want to be the weak link [Laughs]. I just wanted to do my best.

MH: Speaking of John and Sam, you guys have this very lived-in dynamic—did you have to work ahead of time to achieve that?

SB: I’ve known Sam for like 35 years. I don’t always get to see him, but whenever I do, we just kind of fall back to that easiness of, we just really like each other and want to know what we’re up to. We have a lot of mutual friends, and people we’ve worked with. I don’t know John as well, but I have worked with him, and naturally, the scene we did in the office, that’s the only scene I have with John, we filmed that later. So, I got to hang out with him quite a bit when we were in Rapa Nui, on Easter Island. We were all staying at the same resort, so it was very easy to run into each other. And everything about our relationship—not that I have such a big relationship with John—but I’ve known him for a long time, I really respect him, I admire him, and I think I would be very intimidated if I were to direct him. So imagining that I would be his boss [laughs], it’s like, you want his respect, but if he doesn’t give it to you, how crushing that would be? I think that really served my character well.

MH: Is there any sort of mental hurdle to that? Like, locking in and being like ‘I am John Malkovich’s boss.”

SB: You just dive in. The material is so good, and John is so nice. He’s not an actor that will play games with you on set, or try and intimidate you. He’s just so open and easy to work with. I love that, because then you can really feel like ‘Oh, if I go at him hard, it’s OK.’ Or if he goes at me hard, it’s fine. We’re not going to take it personally.

MH: Does shooting on location in Chile come with a different experience?

SB: In Santiago, we were shooting in the square where the coup happened. So, that was very powerful just to imagine that this is where those real events happened. Then shooting on Easter Island, Rapa Nui… that’s a magical place. The horses really do run wild, and the dogs run wild, and there’s chickens and cows, and the Moai are incredible, and the history there. Like I said, we were all staying in the same resort, so it was just all about the work. There weren’t any real distractions. Everywhere you look informed the work that we were doing.

MH: Did you do any kind of research ahead of time?

SB: I asked Martin, “What should I be reading?” and he suggested this one book on the CIA that was very… I don’t know, it got very in the weeds, and the main gist of it—I couldn’t even finish it because it was just so disheartening to know what the CIA had been up to. But there’s a lot of big egos, and maybe that was the main thing I took away from it. I think MJ is very ambitious, and I love the outfits that he wore. That was one thing that really informed me—like, OK, this guy thinks a lot of himself. That’s good. I think I had first envisioned him as more of a bureaucrat in simple suits. So, I like that about the character, and I liked that he had that other dimension to him, where he had that coping mechanism. That, to me, made him very human.

MH: Is it fun to bounce back and forth between genres?

SB: As an actor, I’ve always wanted to sort of be able to do it all, and not be confined to any one genre. When I first started out, I did a lot of independent films, which I loved. It was in the late ’80s and ’90s, and I think that was a time when independent film was really coming into its own. But I never wanted to just be known as, like, “the independent film guy.” I wanted to do other things. I wanted to work in studio movies, comedies, TV—and I feel very lucky that I’ve been able to explore and get work in all these different types of films, and I don’t feel constricted to be any one way or to do any one thing.

MH: Does that inform how you approach and play your latest roles?

SB: I don’t know that it informs how I would do it, but I think, certainly, maybe the work that Martin has seen me do in the past, like with the Coen brothers, or Quentin, or Boardwalk, made him think I was right for this character. Whatever the reason he thought of me, I’m happy [laughs].

MH: Was there any kind of bump in the road along the way on Wild Horse Nine?

SB: Not really a bump in the road, but just wanting to do my best. I have a monologue at the end, where I’m telling Sam’s character the way things are going to go, and the way things are, and I knew that was an important part of the story, and part of MJ’s character. And you just want to get it right. These days, I have to spend more time on the script, and learning the lines, and getting it right, than when I was younger, when I used to just be able to pick things up very quickly. It takes more work now, but it’s OK [laughs].

MH: At the end of the day, Wild Horse Nine is a story of close friendship between these two guys who would do anything for each other. Is there anyone in the industry who’s like that for you?

SB: I have a very old friend, Mark Boone Junior, and we used to write and perform our own theater pieces, and I feel like it was with him that I learned a lot about being an actor. I also learned how to write, because he and I used to write our own shows, and also, by default, we would direct ourselves. So, that was the beginning of me directing. I feel like I owe a lot to him, and that time in my life. We live on different coasts and we don’t see each other a lot, but whenever we do, we just always fall back into that ease of friendship. He’s somebody I would do anything for, or anything that he asked of me.

MH: I have to end with a question about The Big Lebowski, which is my favorite movie of all time. Because not only have I just seen you in Wild Horse Nine, but I just saw John Goodman in Digger, and you’re also in Only Living Pickpocket in New York with John Turturro. What kind of relationship do you all have these days?

SB: I was very happy to work with John Turturro on Pickpocket, because I hadn’t worked with him in years and years. We know each other—I don’t live in Brooklyn anymore, but we used to live in the same neighborhood. I see him around town, or at screenings, or festivals. And so it was so satisfying and gratifying to be able to do a movie with him in New York.

Goodman, I haven’t seen in a while, but a few years ago, I don’t know what anniversary it was of The Big Lebowski, but me, John, and Jeff Bridges sat down for an interview, and then we had dinner afterwards, and it was just… I don’t even think we hung out that much during the making of the movie. But that dinner, that night, we just had the best time. And we were looking at each other, going, ‘Why didn’t we do this while we were making the movie?’ [Laughs] But that’s the nature of it—you know, we were filming in L.A., and when you’re filming in your home town, I mean, I don’t live there, but it’s such a spread out town. You do the work, and then you go off and do other things. That’s what I loved about shooting in Rapa Nui—we were just all there, and so we not only had the great memory of making the movie, but also hanging out together and having dinners, and seeing each other at breakfast.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

#4941. Mercado Bitcoin begins post-hack reopening without a timeline for withdrawals

Customers can view their pre-hack balances as access returns in phases. The exchange has yet to say when withdrawals will resume.

Crypto exchange Mercado Bitcoin began restoring read-only access to customer accounts in phases on Monday, a week after attackers drained an estimated $49 million from the platform. The exchange has yet to say when customers will be able to withdraw funds.

A Mercado Bitcoin spokesperson told Cointelegraph that access would begin at 19:00 UTC, with all accounts expected to be viewable by 21:00 UTC. Customers will be able to see their holdings as they stood on Nov. 2, the day of the hack.

In a video posted to the exchange’s X account, Fabricio Tota, vice president of new business, said Mercado Bitcoin hoped read-only access would reassure customers that their assets were still recorded on the platform.

Mercado Bitcoin, one of Brazil’s largest crypto exchanges, told Brazilian magazine Veja in late October that it had more than 4.8 million customers and had processed over 215 billion Brazilian reais ($39 billion) in cumulative transactions. The exchange says it is using its own funds to replace the stolen assets, but has yet to give a date for withdrawals to resume.

Tota explained that the company opted for “a more conservative approach” to reactivating accounts “to ensure our return happens with the utmost security.” The caution follows concerns from blockchain security analysts that the attackers were able to access addresses, draining funds through standard transfers rather than a smart contract exploit.

Blockchain analysts traced some of the stolen funds to customer deposit addresses, but Mercado Bitcoin says customers won’t bear the losses. “The vast majority of assets, in fact, nearly all of them, were never even touched by anyone; they were not even involved in the incident,” Tota said in the video.

On Nov. 4, Mercado Bitcoin pledged to cover all losses with company funds, bolstered by a $20 million investment from stablecoin issuer Tether in July. Tota highlighted that a portion of the stolen funds were also frozen in the hours after the company announced an attack had taken place.

The next phase of recovery will require users to confirm their registration details on the platform before they can move funds. The exchange plans to restore withdrawals via Pix, Brazil’s instant payment system, initially allowing customers to withdraw only to accounts in their own name. Tota gave no date for either step.

Mercado Bitcoin has yet to explain how the attackers gained access to its wallets. Blockchain data company Bitquery found that as of Nov. 4, wallets it linked to the attacker held most of the stolen funds that had escaped freezes, including 106.07 BTC.

#4934. Volkswagen’s EV battery recall reaches over 85,000, with Audi now included

Volkswagen’s latest recall now extends to Audi, affecting nearly 13,000 Q4 E-Tron and Q4 Sportback E-Tron vehicles globally. And just like with the ID.4, owners are being told to limit charging to 80% of the battery’s capacity.

Earlier this month, Volkswagen issued a recall with the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) impacting 22,524 ID.4 electric SUVs, model years 2023 to 2026.

Official information from the German Federal Motor Transport Authority (KBA) revealed shortly after that several thousand more VW Group electric vehicles are affected worldwide.

In three separate recalls now under investigation by the KBA, 72,668 VW Group EVs were included worldwide, including 9,881 in Germany.

The recall affects certain batches of the ID. Buzz, ID.3, ID.4, and ID.7 models produced between 2022 and 2025, as well as Cupra Born models from 2022 to 2024.

On October 27, VW Group added another electric vehicle to the KBA recall database. The latest recall impacts 12,734 Audi Q4 E-Tron and Q4 Sportback E-Tron vehicles globally, including just one in Germany. In total, Volkswagen’s battery recall now affects 85,402 EVs globally.

In the letter sent to the NHTSA on October 23, Volkswagen Group of America said it was issuing a recall affecting 9,048 Audi Q4 E-Tron and Q4 Sportback E-Tron vehicles in the US model years 2024 to 2025.

The recall stems from the risk of the battery overheating and potentially causing a fire under certain conditions when charged to over 80% of its limit.

Volkswagen said in a safety recall report with the NHTSA that “certain charging profiles combined with a state of charge over 80% may trigger thermal propagation.”

While the root cause is unknown, Volkswagen urged owners to limit charging to 80%. Owners of affected vehicles received an email saying VW “intends to apply a temporary vehicle setting that limits charging to 80%,” unless they opted out by October 21.

The safety recall follows Audi’s Product Safety Committee being informed of battery thermal events involving other Volkswagen Group EVs that use the same batteries as the Audi Q4 E-Tron.

Following an internal investigation, Audi found the same battery and production characteristics as the impacted VW Group vehicles. Audi said the majority of these were produced for the North American market.

Audi is not aware of any claims, crashes, fires, injuries, or deaths associated with the Q4 E-Tron’s battery issue.

Dang it. I posted this concern in the other article that covered the ID.4 that Audi Q4 would be next. And here we are. I guess I'm going to have to call the hotline and see if mine is part of the recall. This is starting out just like the Chevy Bolt fiasco. I had a 2020 Bolt that eventually got a new battery pack but it was a battle with GM till they finally got it done. My guess is the VW group will do the same thing here. And oh by the way selling the car while this recall is in question will be a no go. Ugghh.

The root cause has yet to be determined, and until a remedy is available, Audi is advising owners to limit charging to 80%.

Owners in the US can take their vehicles to an authorized Audi dealer, where the dealer will run a diagnostic check on the battery. If needed, the dealer will impose a temporary state-of-charge limit to 80%.

Audi expects to mail out interim letters notifying owners of the safety risks on December 18, 2026. Additional letters will send additional letters once a remedy is available.

Owners can contact Audi’s customer service at 1-800-253-2834 with questions or concerns. Audi’s recall number is 93SK. You can also contact the NHTSA Hotline at 1-888-327-4236 or visit nhtsa.gov for more information. The NHTSA campaign number is 26V698.

#4930. Formation Securities plans Solana stock trading venue under SEC exemption

The proposed venue would offer 24/7 trading of tokenized shares from Galaxy Digital, Exodus and Forward Industries, with a launch planned for early 2027.

Formation Securities, a newly established company licensing technology from decentralized exchange Orca, has notified the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) of plans to launch a tokenized stock trading venue on Solana in early 2027.

The company said Monday that tokenized shares of Galaxy Digital, Exodus Movement and Forward Industries are expected to be available at launch. The venue would operate under the SEC’s temporary Innovation Exemption announced in September, which allows verified investors to trade certain US-listed shares around the clock while retaining voting, dividend and other shareholder rights.

Superstate and Securitize are expected to make tokenized shares available on the marketplace, which Formation says it intends to develop in cooperation with public companies. The venue would use automated market maker (AMM) technology, allowing investors to hold securities in their own wallets and settle trades onchain against stablecoins and other eligible assets.

Traditional transfer agents would continue to maintain official shareholder records, preserving companies’ direct relationships with shareholders.

Solana is already among the largest blockchains for tokenized equities, with approximately $575 million in tokenized stock value as of Monday, compared with $747 million on Ethereum and $1.7 billion on BNB Chain, according to RWA.xyz data.

Related: Robinhood’s Tenev warns US tokenization uncertainty deters ‘serious assets’

Formation’s issuer-focused approach comes amid growing debate over how publicly traded shares should be brought onchain. As of early November, more than 50 companies had objected to having their shares tokenized on OKXICE, a proposed venue backed by crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange.

Under the SEC’s Innovation Exemption, trading venues must notify issuers in writing and wait at least 30 calendar days before trading shares tokenized by unaffiliated third parties, giving companies an opportunity to object. Nasdaq warned in September that tokenized stock trading could have unforeseen consequences for liquidity, price discovery and long-term shareholder value.

#4928. Dems introduce new $15,000 EV tax credit bill that has no chance — and that’s a problem

Rep. Greg Landsman (D-OH) wants to bring back the federal EV tax credit and double it. His new bill, the “21st Century Vehicle Choice Act” (H.R. 10881), would offer up to $15,000 on a new electric vehicle and $8,000 on a used one, and it would make both credits permanent.

The problem is that the bill has virtually no chance of becoming law anytime soon. And if EV shoppers start holding out for it, it could end up hurting the market it’s trying to help.

Landsman introduced H.R. 10881 on October 22. It was referred to three House committees (Ways and Means, Energy and Commerce, and Natural Resources), and as of today, it doesn’t have a single cosponsor.

The big change is to the new clean vehicle credit, Section 30D of the tax code. Under the Inflation Reduction Act, the $7,500 credit came in two $3,750 halves: one for meeting critical mineral sourcing requirements and one for battery components. Landsman’s bill doubles each half to $7,500, for a maximum of $15,000.

It also deletes the termination dates. The One Big Beautiful Bill Act killed the new and used EV credits for vehicles acquired after September 30, 2025, and the home charger credit after June 30, 2026. Striking those dates would revive all three credits with no expiration.

The bill doesn’t touch the rest of 30D. North American final assembly, the $55,000 MSRP cap on cars and $80,000 cap on SUVs, pickups, and vans, and the foreign entity of concern rules all stay. So the full $15,000 would only go to EVs that clear both sourcing tests, which has always been a short list.

The rest of the bill is reports. Commerce would deliver a “Vehicle Choice Report” on vehicle variety and ownership costs, and the Department of Energy would file five years of critical minerals reports.

The most interesting part is a DOE study of “gasoline super-users,” defined as the top 10% of light-duty vehicle owners by gas consumption. The study would map the regions where they’re concentrated and their 100 most-used routes, then track charger availability along those routes every year for five years. Coltura, which backs the bill, says the highest-mileage drivers spend over 10% of household income on fuel.

Republicans control both the House and the Senate, and they’re the ones who just repealed these credits. President Trump signed that repeal.

Even if Democrats flip the House and Senate in next week’s midterms, a bill restoring EV credits would still need to get through a veto. So realistically, nothing like this happens before 2029.

It’s also a rerun. Landsman introduced the exact same “21st Century Vehicle Choice Act” as H.R. 10046 on October 25, 2024, 11 days before that year’s election. It got zero cosponsors and died in committee. This version landed 12 days before the November 3 midterms.

This is where the bill can do real damage. EV buyers are extremely sensitive to tax credit timing, and the last year proved it.

When the credit was about to disappear, buyers rushed in, and Q3 2025 became the best quarter ever for US EV sales, with market share topping 10% for the first time. Then the floor dropped out. Hyundai’s Ioniq 5 sales fell 63% the month after the credit ended.

The market is only now crawling back. According to Cox Automotive, US EV sales hit 262,649 units in Q3 2026, up 6.1% from Q2 but still down 39.5% year-over-year, at a 6.4% share of new vehicle sales. Tesla’s Model Y and Model 3 still dominate, but Toyota, Rivian, and Hyundai are growing again.

That recovery is fragile. A headline promising “$15,000 off your next EV” is exactly what makes a shopper on the fence decide to wait a year or two.

And waiting gets you nothing. The new credits would only apply to vehicles placed in service after the bill is enacted, so there’s no retroactive payout for buying now. If you already claimed the credit once, you’d get $0 anyway.

Meanwhile, the deals on the lot are real. Automakers have been cutting prices to make up for the lost credit, like Hyundai, which cut the 2026 Ioniq 5 by nearly $10,000 on some trims.

At Electrek, our position has always been clear: we think EVs are a net positive and ICE vehicles have unpriced externalities. Therefore, we will take whatever we can get to encourage EV sales, but our preferred approach would be a carbon tax on ICE vehicles.

We’ve been clear that policy shapes this market. Killing the $7,500 credit is the main reason US EV sales collapsed. Consumers didn’t suddenly decide they hate EVs. So we’re all for bringing incentives back.

But this bill is messaging. It has zero cosponsors and no CBO score, and it dropped two weeks before an election, just like the identical version two years ago.

They should focus on rooftop and community solar tax credits and initiatives. With rising electric rates, helping people alleviate those would be vastly more popular than EV subsidies.

To be fair, some of it is smart. The lower income caps and the once-per-taxpayer limit go after the most common criticism of the old credit, that it subsidized wealthy households buying their second or third EV. And the “super-user” study is the best idea in the bill. Getting a high-mileage driver out of a gas pickup does far more for fuel costs and emissions than putting a low-mileage commuter in their second Tesla. If Democrats want to rebuild EV policy in 2029, that’s where they should start.

The problem is that people will hear the headline number. If you need a car now and an EV fits your life, buy it now and take the deals that are on the table. Don’t plan your purchase around a bill that has no path to passing for at least two years.

Will Democrats come back with a real, cosponsored EV plan before 2028? I don’t know, but I suspect that the US will continue to fall behind the rest of the world when it comes to EV adoption.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero, with or without a tax credit. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. #4925. North Korean hackers create memecoins to launder funds from Binance hack: Elliptic

The blockchain firm traced stolen funds through six new tokens on NEAR, where hackers used liquidity pools to obscure transfers between wallets.

North Korean hackers created memecoins to launder cryptocurrency stolen in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, routing roughly $430,000 through six newly launched tokens on the NEAR blockchain, according to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

Between Nov. 5 and 7, the hackers created at least six tokens on NEAR and used liquidity pools on decentralized exchanges to move stolen funds between wallets, Elliptic said. The tokens attracted almost no outside trading and were abandoned shortly after launch.

The hackers used one set of wallets to swap stolen NEAR tokens for the new memecoins, then used another set to sell those memecoins back into the pools for NEAR. The swaps let them move funds without direct wallet-to-wallet transfers, though the transactions remained traceable onchain.

How stolen NEAR moved between wallets through liquidity pools. Source: Elliptic

Elliptic also found that five of the six token creators were funded with proceeds from earlier token sales just two to 13 minutes before the next launch, linking the tokens in a chain of transactions.

“North Korea’s launderers rarely rely on a single technique,” Elliptic co-founder Tom Robinson wrote in the report. “Instead, they layer many cheap techniques, each adding a little more delay.”

Elliptic said the same laundering technique was also used following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025, when North Korean-linked actors moved at least $24 million through a newly created token on the TRON blockchain.

At the time of Elliptic’s analysis, about 11.8 million NEAR stolen in the Binance hack remained unmoved. Elliptic valued those funds at $43.7 million.

#4922. Should you leave Australia to avoid paying 47% tax on crypto gains?

Some Australian crypto investors are moving overseas to avoid the huge CGT increase from July 2027. But there are also less drastic ways you can pay less tax.

In part one of our special on how to avoid Australia’s massive hike in capital gains taxes on crypto in July next year, we looked at borrowing against your Bitcoin or setting up a self-managed super fund (SMSF), among four ways to mitigate the impact of the taxes.While an SMSF offers Australians the greatest tax advantages, it’s only suitable for older investors, or those who are willing to lock up their crypto for decades until they retire. For younger investors, moving overseas could reduce your future crypto tax bills to 0% — but there are less drastic options to save on tax too.

Australia’s impending tax changes have already inspired crypto king Fred Schebesta, founder of Finder.com, to become a tax exile in Singapore.

From July, the top rate on real, long-term crypto gains will increase to 47%, minus the effects of inflation on the cost basis.

For upwardly mobile, single crypto investors, the “nuclear option” of leaving the country to avoid giving almost half their gains to the government will look increasingly attractive.Many of the world’s top crypto hubs — Singapore, Portugal, Malta, UAE and Hong Kong — offer 0% capital gains taxes on long-term crypto gains in an attempt to attract wealthy investors.

Also read: The one thing these 6 global crypto hubs all have in common...Should you leave Australia to avoid paying 47% tax on crypto gains?

Ben Simpson, founder of crypto advisory service Collective Shift, says some of his members have already pulled the trigger.

“Yeah, we’ve had a number of people that have sort of packed up and gone basically, ‘f—- this. I’m off to the Bahamas or whatnot,’” he says.

Andrea Yuen, CEO of Sywftx, says she is keenly aware of the tax advantages of living outside of Australia. “I lived in Singapore for 30 years. I do miss my no capital gains tax,” she says. But she didn’t move there for tax reasons, and cautions that uprooting your life to avoid a bit of tax is a pretty drastic step.

“I would never say that we should structure our lives only to think about tax outcomes,” she says. “There are many other wonderful reasons why I’ve come back to Australia and continued to live in Australia.”

Simpson has considered the idea, given he already spends half the year in America looking after Collective Shift US, but looking into the rules put him off. “You really have to cut pretty much all ties to Australia. So if you are genuinely happy to leave and don’t want to come back for the majority of the year, and cut your healthcare and cut all your ties back to Australia, then that is a strategy.”

“Bitcoin Jesus” Roger Ver gave up his US residency in 2014, and ten years later he was arrested and charged over failing to correctly report the value of his crypto holdings and paying exit taxes. He was forced to pay almost $50 million to make the case go away. Similarly, Australians leaving the country face a hefty exit tax on departure, which may require selling a decent amount of your stack.

“They tax you on the way out. If you’ve got a lot of gains or profits you’re sitting on, they’ll catch you when you leave,” says Adrian Forza, director of Crypto Tax Australia. “Arguably it’d be bigger in the future, but it’s still difficult to stomach selling off everything to leave.”

Forza explains you also have the option of paying the Australian taxes later on, but on the full value of the gain after you sell the asset. So if you expect Bitcoin to go up, you’d be best off paying the exit tax now.

Related: Australia releases draft bill to adopt OECD crypto tax reporting framework

Simpson is only 27 and the prospect of locking up his crypto portfolio for forty years holds little appeal. Instead, he trades and holds crypto via a company to benefit from the 25% company tax rate.

Running a single-person company is easier than you might think, but not worth the hassle unless you have a reasonable amount of capital and intend to make a lot of trades. It requires registering the company with ASIC and paying an annual fee, paying an accountant to produce financial statements and/or an annual company tax return and mucking about with payroll software and payday superannuation contributions if you plan to pay yourself an income. You can pay yourself a dividend at any time when you need some money.

“The company structure is good if you’re wanting to cap the capital gains tax at the company tax rate,” he says, but notes there’s a big catch: when you take funds out of the company, you need to pay the difference between the 25% company tax rate and your personal tax rate. “You still need to pay your marginal income tax rates. If you’re on the top tax rate, like 47%,” he says.

You may be wondering: if you have to pay your income tax rate anyway, which will equal the CGT rate from July, what is the benefit in paying a few thousand dollars and filling out a bunch of paperwork to run a company?

Simpson explains that the key advantage is that while you are building up your crypto portfolio by trading in and out of different coins, you only pay the 25% company rate for each sale. That means paying up to 22% less than CGT each time you exit a position, which builds into an even more significant saving over time.

“Instead of paying 47% because you’ve just taken the profits… then the CGT amount is only 25% — the company tax rate. So when you put that money back in, you’ve got more capital to play with.”

However, if you don’t trade enough, it might be taxed at 30%.

Forza says the advantages are greater for those on higher incomes.

It’s not a particularly useful structure if you just plan to just hold your Bitcoin, however. There are some potential advantages but arguably not enough of them to be worth the hassle.

The 50% CGT discount has been replaced by an indexation discount, which takes into account how much inflation affects how much you paid originally for an asset.

This means that investors who buy and hold dividend stocks or index funds for a long period will receive a bigger CGT discount. So should you drop crypto and buy a bunch of ETFs so you can pay less tax?

The short answer is no: the new CGT ‘discount’ is largely an illusion, as it simply measures your real gain after inflation.

Simpson says crypto has been hit hard by the CGT changes, but it still offers the best risk-reward opportunity.

History suggests that despite volatility and years-long bear markets, the return from investing in Bitcoin over time would still leave investors ahead of ETF investors even after paying more tax. Investing further down the risk curve with altcoins however, can either pay off handsomely or end in total disaster.

JPMorgan assumes that Bitcoin will compound by 10% on average in the years ahead. (Source JPMorgan/Bitcoin Archive)

Simpson says he convinced his younger sister, who is a basketball player on a low income, to invest in Bitcoin two years ago rather than putting money into a savings account. “She’s up like 40% or 50% in her portfolio and she’s like, ‘wow, this is going to get me so much further towards buying a house one day than sitting in cash and losing 10% [to inflation over time],” he says.

“That’s why I think Bitcoin makes so much sense to accumulate. And the tax considerations are not really there for most people I think.”

Yuen says crypto is one of the few asset classes that offers investors the chance to make double-digit returns in a few months, or to potentially double their money. Even after paying the new taxes, crypto investors with those sorts of returns will still be ahead.

“My dad always said to me, it’s a good thing, if you’ve made profits, you should be happy to pay some tax on it.”

Well maybe not happy about it, but you’ll still be making money.

#4921. Used EVs are suddenly hot sellers — Here are the top SUVs for under $25,000

Don’t want to pay full price? Used EVs with low miles, like the Hyundai IONIQ 5 and Ford Mustang Mach-E, are selling on the secondhand market for $25,000 or less.

With a flood of electric cars returning from lease this year and soaring gas prices, used EVs are suddenly flying off the lot.

Used EV sales rose to a record 3.3% of total used vehicle sales last month, up from 2.9% in August, according to Cox Automotive.

Used ICE vehicle sales, on the other hand, dropped 4.4% in September as buyers flocked to more efficient options.

As expected, Tesla remains the leading used EV brand, followed by Ford, Chevrolet, Hyundai, and BMW.

By model, the Nissan Ariya led growth, with sales up 42.5% in September, followed by the Chevy Equinox EV (+41.9%), Kia EV9 (+36.2%), and Chevy Blazer EV (+30.2%), while the Ford Mustang Mach-E (+14.7%) and Tesla Model Y (+13.8 %) also saw more demand.

With more options to choose from, which one is best for you? To help you decide, Edmunds, which has tested nearly all of them, put together a list of four top used electric SUVs available for under $25,000.

The first one on the list was the Chevy Equinox EV, or as GM used to call it, “America’s most affordable 315+ range” electric vehicle.

After it arrived in 2024, the Chevy Equinox EV quickly became one of the top-selling electric vehicles in the US outside Tesla, winning over buyers with enough space for a family and plenty of driving range at an affordable price.

The Chevy Equinox EV offers up to 319 miles of EPA-estimated driving range. It’s a great fit for those looking for a smaller, more efficient SUV that still offers plenty of interior space.

Edmunds says it’s worth upgrading to the 2LT trim, which adds heated front seats and a heated steering wheel, a wireless phone charger, adaptive cruise control, and a 360-degree HD Surround Vision camera system.

Next on the list was the Ford Mustang Mach-E, which has also remained a top seller since it went on sale in 2021.

Ford tapped into its iconic Mustang nameplate to help attract new buyers. The Mustang Mach-E came well equipped with a large 15.5″ infotainment screen with Wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto and Ford Co-Pilot360 Assist+.

While the GT is the most fun to drive, Edmunds said the Premium trim may be your best bet. It still offers plenty of performance while adding standard features like heated front seats and a heated steering wheel.

It’s also worth upgrading to the extended-range battery pack, which offers up to 320 miles of range on the Premium RWD model. You should be able to find used 2021 to 2023 Mach-E’s for under $25,000.

Another top-selling electric SUV, the Hyundai IONIQ 5 continues to win over buyers with its bold design, long-range capabilities, fast charging, and a surprisingly spacious interior.

Edmunds says plenty of used 2022 to 2024 IONIQ 5s with low mileage are available for under $25,000.

While the base SE Standard Range is the cheapest, it only offers an EPA range of 220 miles. Upgrading to the Extended Range battery, which is used on all other trims, provides up to 303 miles of range.

The Volkswagen ID.4 went on sale in the US in 2021 as one of the earlier mainstream electric SUVs.

It also just happens that many EVs are coming off lease so the inventory is plentiful combined with high gas price. Perfect storm.

It was updated in 2024, but up until the 2023 model year, the VW ID.4 offered an EPA range of up to 275 miles, depending on the configuration.

Finding a low-mileage used 2023 ID.4 shouldn’t be hard, but upgrading to the Pro S trim might be worth it.

Volkswagen updated the 2023 ID.4 with a new 12″ infotainment display and Park Assist Plus. The Pro S trim gained a fixed panoramic roof, V-Tex synthetic leather seating, and a heated steering wheel.

#4918. Dutch startup Elysian says electric flight may be closer than you think

They day you get to book an all-electric flight may be closer than you think if Dutch startup Elysian Aircraft gets its way. Their new E9X commercial airliner promises flights that are a whole lot quieter, and at prices that are whole lot less dependent on dumb oil wars.

Aircraft account for more than 2% of global heating when CO₂ and other emissions are taken into account, with the share considerably higher in wealthy countries, according to data cited by climate campaign group Stay Grounded. Elysian Aircraft is keenly aware of the industry’s climate impact, and they believe their planned full-size electric airliner will be perfectly positioned to make a dramatic difference.

“By being able to transport 90 passengers for initially 800 kilometers and eventually 1,000 kilometers you can reduce emissions to zero, from half the world’s flights,” Elysian co-founder Daniel Jacobson told EuroNews. “That is a very large amount of flight activity in the world. And you reduce total emissions from aerospace by 20%.”

At the core of those ambitious emission-reduction goals is the Elysian E9X aircraft. Designed in collaboration with aerospace researchers at Delft University of Technology (TU Delft), the E9X packs its oversized wings with modular battery packs, sending power to six propellers driven by electric motors.

Specs on those haven’t been revealed, but the E9X is designed to carry up to 100 passengers up to 1,000 km (~620 miles) from where they started. Elysian says that’s enough range to handle nearly 50% of scheduled commercial passenger flights worldwide and potentially eliminate 20% of total in-flight emissions when the plane enters service in 2034.

That said, the E9X is far more than “just a concept,” and Elysian has completed its second conceptual design review and flown a scaled demonstrator in concert with Germany’s DLR aerospace research center, demonstrating how its unique battery packaging solutions address the challenges of integrating heavy battery packs into an aircraft’s wings (where fuel is traditionally stored).

It’s interesting to see all the comments that point out the difficulties and problems associated with the introduction of battery powered aircraft into the mainstream industry. What none of them address is the industry’s obsession with cutting their operating costs no matter what pain passengers and flight crews must endure. My bet is if the airlines can save money by converting to battery powered aircraft—there are NO obstacles that will stop them from doing it. Battery electric aircraft may reduce operating costs by thousands of dollars per flight. That alone tells me that they will be flying passengers as soon as possible.

The company is also working with airline partners KLM and Transavia to explore the operational and commercial requirements for putting electric airliners into service, with aircraft lessor TrueNoord investigating how the E9X could be financed and supported commercially.

#4917. Jessica McClain's Approach to Running Breaks a Lot of the Usual Rules. It's Working.

The Brooks athlete spoke to Women’s Health fresh off the NYC Marathon.

After a major marathon, you hear about two different podiums. There’s the actual podium, made up of the top three finishers, and then another, unofficial one of the fastest Americans. That’s because, though there’s some incredible talent in the U.S., oftentimes, it’s simply not enough to beat out international runners, including on our own courses. In the New York City Marathon women’s race, for example, an American hasn’t been in the top three for eight years. (In the men’s race, it’s been since 2009.)

That streak ended this Sunday when Brooks athlete Jessica McClain crossed the finish line in 2:24:42, coming in at second place. Even more exciting: She was poised to get third but overtook last year’s winner Hellen Obiri in the final meters. She did it looking effortless, with a pair of Goodr sunglasses in her hand and a huge smile on her face.

Even before the race cannon went off that morning, her momentum had been building. In the spring, McClain placed fifth in the Boston Marathon at 2:20:49 and set an American course record. Going into New York, she initially wanted to be in the top five again. “But then I was like, ‘Wait, I want to do better than I did in Boston, and that's fourth, and I do not want fourth place,’” she tells Women’s Health. So, it was settled: She was shooting for third or higher.

After a performance like she had in New York, you can expect that’s always going to be the goal now for the 34-year-old. “I think ‘top American’ just isn't going to cut it anymore for where I want to be on the international stage,” she says. “I want to compete and I want to practice competing, especially because we're coming up on the Olympic trials, and the top three go to the Olympics. I want to practice competing for those top three spots no matter the race.”

Heading into NYC, McClain was feeling relaxed. The race started out hard, she remembers, so she just focused on getting her fuel in at each aid station and taking the run mile by mile. “I was just checking boxes and staying out of trouble as much as I could the first 13 to 16 miles,” she says. Then, once she got to Manhattan, the pack started to run faster, her gels started to kick in, and she started to feel better. Still, it wasn’t until the last 10K when the running started to feel really good.

McClain was strategic until the end—running the tangents and taking the shortest turns possible to close the gap between Obiri and first-place finisher Sheila Chepkirui, who she noticed were making their turns a little wider and slower. Even down to the last stretch turning into Central Park, second wasn’t exactly on McClain’s mind. She was just thrilled to take third. She took off her sunglasses to take it all in, which is why they were in her hand as she crossed the finish line.

“Then I saw Hellen looking around and was like, ‘Oh my God, wait, I think I can get second,’” she says. So, she dug deep, pumped her arms, and fed off the crowd’s energy. “I just felt really, really good,” she says. “I've run a lot of reps at the end of a lot of hard workouts that were fast and I was like, ‘I can do this.’” The result was one of the most exciting second-place finishes to a marathon in a long time.

Despite her headline-grabbing performances in 2026, it was just a few years ago that McClain stepped away from professional running after hitting what she describes as “rock bottom.” She was chronically injured, having dealt with her share of tight tendons, stress fractures, plantar issues, and strained muscles, and unable to race at her full potential. So, she moved home to Arizona, got a full-time job in the nonprofit sector, and reevaluated her relationship with running.

“It gave me a lot of perspective,” McClain says. “For a long time I was very outcome oriented, not process driven. It was a lot about ‘We need to hit these times and get this place,’ and I wasn't enjoying the ride to get there, and it trickled into a lot of other things in life.”

From 2020 to 2022, McClain just ran for herself. She didn’t have a super formal plan or even a coach. She worked full time, met the man who is now her husband, reconnected with old friends, and had fun outside of her sport. “I needed that, but naturally found my way back [to running],” she says. She signed up for the Mesa Marathon in February 2022 to celebrate her 30th birthday—and won it.

Slowly, she started racing again, even self-coaching her way to a fourth-place performance in the 2024 Olympic marathon trials. Now, she’s resigned with Brooks and back to racing as a professional, but she still maintains all those other parts of herself she gained in her time off. That period away from professional running didn’t sabotage her progress. Instead, it set her up for this latest era, one where she’s happier—and more successful—than ever.

Stepping away from professional running led to a mindset shift for McClain. First, she prioritizes fun—and you can tell. Obviously, coming off of an incredible finish at one of the world’s biggest marathons will put you in a good mood, but it’s clear that McClain is enthusiastic about running. She leads with such a pure love for the sport that pros (and amateurs) can lose sight of, that she had once lost sight of until her time away.

“This is supposed to be fun,” McClain says. “Running is amazing, but at the end of the day, it's just running and there's so much else in life. I just want to enjoy it and to get the best out of myself and make the sport a little bit better, whatever that means.”

That philosophy carries over into her day-to-day training, where she prioritizes workouts she actually enjoys. “Eighty-five percent of the work I do gives me confidence, and then there's 15 percent that's really hard that I don't love, but that's good for me,” McClain says. She’s also not a cross-trainer. “It does not bring me joy,” McClain says. “I hate swimming. I hate biking.” Yes, even if she gets injured. When it comes to strength training, she keeps that minimal too.

Similarly, you’ll rarely catch McClain wrapping up a session doubled over and exhausted, she says. “I’m never hitting a home run in practice,” she says. “That’s for racing.”

Due to her full-time job as the executive director at the Johnjay & Rich #LoveUp Foundation, her weekly mileage also leans a little shorter than some of her competitors. (During this training block, McClain ran about 72 to 84 miles every week, with one hard session and one long run per week in addition to her easy miles.) Her coach, David Roche, also doesn’t assign runs based on paces. Instead, they focus on effort.

Beyond keeping training from getting too draining, McClain has seen another incredible benefit from this approach: She’s been injury-free for nearly four years. “That's something that just gives me a little bit of an edge,” she says. “I've had a lot of healthy blocks strung together.”

When it comes to nutrition, McClain takes the same refreshingly uncomplicated approach, and it’s paying off. “I just feel like I’m the strongest athlete I’ve ever been,” she says. It’s all very intuitive when it comes to deciding what to eat. You won’t catch her planning out meals or counting macros. There’s nothing super strict or specific to her diet. She eats what she wants and relies on snacks to keep her well-fed. “I’m never hungry,” she says.

Plus, her fueling plan doesn’t come in between her and living life. “In the off season and when my season isn't super crazy, I go out with friends, I have fun, I go to happy hour. I don't try to live in restriction or super high monitoring ever,” McClain says. When she gets closer to race day, she dials in a bit more, but since she’s been so relaxed otherwise, it just makes it fun to lock in for a month.

There is one food that’s become a mainstay in her diet: Burgers—two to three nights a week. “It's super easy,” McClain says. “Throw it on the grill, slap it between two buns and put whatever you want on it. It's great and I get a ton of protein.” Her sweet treat of choice is Dairy Queen, a spot she frequents.

Next, McClain has her sights set on the Olympics. She placed fourth at the last trials, making her an alternate for Paris but just missing the chance to race for the country.

In the meantime, as she waits for 2028, she’s going to keep crushing the marathon and competing for every podium. “It's taken a long time to get to the point where I look at these women—and they're women I've been watching and idolizing in the marathon for quite some time—and I'm like, ‘Oh no, I can race with them too,’” she says. But now, there’s no doubting that she can.

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

#4915. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Elliptic said North Korean hackers used memecoins to launder funds stolen in the Binance hack, EU authorities asked the finance industry to develop common standards for tokenized securities, and Zcash developers proposed cutting block times from 25 to 20 seconds.

North Korean hackers created memecoins to launder cryptocurrency stolen in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, routing roughly $430,000 through six newly launched tokens on the NEAR blockchain, according to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

Between Nov. 5 and 7, the hackers created at least six tokens on NEAR and used liquidity pools on decentralized exchanges to move stolen funds between wallets, Elliptic said. The tokens attracted almost no outside trading and were abandoned shortly after launch.

The hackers used one set of wallets to swap stolen NEAR tokens for the new memecoins, then used another set to sell those memecoins back into the pools for NEAR. The swaps let them move funds without direct wallet-to-wallet transfers, though the transactions remained traceable onchain.

Elliptic said the same laundering technique was also used following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025, when North Korean-linked actors moved at least $24 million through a newly created token on the TRON blockchain.

The European Central Bank, European Commission and European Securities and Markets Authority are seeking nominations for an industry-led group that will draft common standards for holding and settling tokenized securities.

Eligible applicants include EU industry associations, global institutions, private firms and fintech companies. They can apply to the European Tokenisation Standards Group (ETSG) by Nov. 27, with work set to begin in January 2027, according to a release published Monday. The three EU institutions will participate as observers.

The group will develop technical and market standards to help companies hold and settle tokenized securities across different distributed ledger technology (DLT) platforms and ensure those platforms can work with existing financial systems. The group aims to produce an initial set of standards by early 2028, according to its mandate. The standards will support Appia, the Eurosystem’s initiative to develop a blueprint for Europe’s tokenized financial markets.

The institutions warned that fragmentation could limit the benefits of tokenization and said the group may recommend ways for EU authorities to encourage firms to adopt its standards.

In September, the Eurosystem launched Pontes, a system that allows financial institutions to settle wholesale transactions in tokenized assets in central bank money.

Zcash developers have proposed reducing the blockchain’s target block interval from 25 seconds to 20 seconds, seeking faster transaction confirmations days after activating the NU7 upgrade.

On Sunday, developers ValarDragon and Evan Forbes submitted a draft Zcash Improvement Proposal (ZIP) on GitHub, saying that they want the change to be included in the next community poll and considered for the upcoming NU7.1 network upgrade.

The reduction would shorten the expected wait for initial transaction confirmations. According to the proposal, this would improve the user experience for point-of-sale payments, exchange deposits and cross-chain swaps.

The developers said the change would not increase the network’s overall daily capacity for private transactions. The proposal would also preserve Zcash’s existing issuance schedule, despite blocks being produced more frequently.

The proposal comes just days after Zcash’s NU7 upgrade went live. On Friday, the NU7 upgrade was activated on the mainnet, cutting the network’s target block interval from 75 seconds to 25 seconds.

The upgrade also introduced a Network Sustainability Mechanism designed to recycle a portion of transaction fees into future mining rewards while preserving Zcash’s 21 million ZEC supply cap.

#4914. BYD now has 1.02 million workers, more than Toyota, GM, Ford, and Tesla combined

BYD now has more than 1.02 million employees, according to chairman Wang Chuanfu. That’s more people than Toyota, GM, Ford, and Tesla employ combined.

And it’s a big jump from the start of the year. BYD reported about 869,600 employees at the end of 2025, which means the Chinese automaker has added roughly 150,000 people in 10 months while its vehicle sales are down.

Roughly 960,000 of BYD’s people work in China and about 60,000 work abroad. Up to 120,000 of them are R&D engineers, according to Wang, who shared the figures on stage Wednesday at the China Society of Automotive Engineers’ (CSAE) 33rd annual conference. Their work covers battery cells, motors, electronic controls, and semiconductors.

Wang credits vertical integration for that scale. In his telling, keeping batteries, chips, and motors in-house is what took BYD from a battery maker founded in 1994 to the world’s top-selling new energy vehicle brand several years running. He called the company’s rise a microcosm of China’s auto industry going from follower to leader.

BYD’s workforce has been all over the place in the last few years.

The company had about 570,100 employees at the end of 2022 and 703,500 at the end of 2023. Then it went on a hiring spree. We reported in September 2024 that BYD had passed 900,000 employees and claimed the largest R&D workforce of any automaker, with over 110,000 engineers. It ended 2024 with 968,900 people.

Then came the first big cut. BYD’s 2025 annual report showed roughly 869,600 employees at year-end, a drop of about 99,000, mostly in frontline production jobs.

Now it’s back over a million. Data compiled by S&P Global Market Intelligence put BYD at 911,900 employees at the end of June, so the company added more than 100,000 people in about four months.

The gap with legacy automakers is massive. Volkswagen Group, which counts its Chinese joint ventures, reported 662,942 employees at the end of 2025. Toyota had 390,927 as of March 31, 2026, and Stellantis had 258,668 at the end of last year.

In the US, Ford ended 2025 with 169,000 employees and GM with 156,000. Tesla reported a worldwide headcount of 134,785 in its latest 10-K.

Add up Toyota, GM, Ford, and Tesla and you get about 851,000 people. BYD has roughly 170,000 more than that.

But headcount isn’t output. If you divide 2025 global vehicle sales by year-end workforce, BYD comes out far behind:

BYD sold about 4.6 million vehicles last year with 869,600 employees, or 5.3 vehicles per worker. Tesla delivered 1,636,129 vehicles with 134,785 people, about 12 per worker, and Volkswagen Group was at about 13.5. Toyota, with 11.3 million group sales including Daihatsu and Hino, got roughly 29 vehicles out of each employee.

There’s a catch, and it’s a big one. BYD’s headcount isn’t only a car company’s headcount. It includes BYD Electronic, its majority-owned contract manufacturer that assembles devices for other brands and reported 165,000 employees of its own at the end of 2025. It also covers BYD’s battery business, which supplies other automakers, plus its in-house chips, motors, energy storage, and rail transit operations.

In short, BYD’s vertical integration largely drives its high headcount.

BYD sold 3,131,576 vehicles in the first nine months of 2026, down 3.9% year-over-year. September was its best month of the year at 463,561 units, but the company has been stuck in a brutal price war at home and is losing ground to rivals like Geely and Leapmotor. Its first-half revenue (344.8 billion yuan) and net profit (12.3 billion yuan) were both down year-over-year.

All of the growth is overseas. BYD’s international sales hit 1,342,960 units through September, up 92.7%, and they now make up about 43% of its total volume. That’s on top of BYD beating Tesla on pure EV deliveries again in Q2.

Yet only 60,000 of its 1.02 million workers are based outside China. That’s about 6%. So BYD is still building nearly everything at home and shipping it out, even as its factories in Thailand, Brazil, and Hungary come online.

The 1.02 million number makes for a great headline, but we wouldn’t read it as a sign of strength on its own.

BYD doesn't just make cars. It makes a lot of battery and other parts that go into other things such as other automaker's EVs.

It is automakers, auto supplier, battery makers, electronic contract manufacturer all merged into one.

Remember GM used to make refrigerators and satellites in addition to cars at one point.

BYD’s whole model is to make almost everything itself: cells, chips, motors, seats, and a lot more. That’s a big reason it can sell a Seagull for around $10,000 in China and still make money. But it’s a labor-heavy model, and it works because Chinese factory labor is relatively cheap and plentiful. Toyota gets more than five times as many cars out of each employee. Tesla gets more than twice as many.

The problem is that BYD’s growth is now outside China, and that’s where this model gets tested. Only 6% of its workforce is abroad, yet 43% of its sales are. Tariffs in Europe, Brazil, Mexico, and elsewhere are pushing BYD to build locally, but a factory in Hungary or Brazil won’t necessarily produce the same.

The number that should worry legacy automakers is the 120,000 engineers. That’s almost as many people as Tesla employs in total, all working on product.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4913. HIVE’s Bitcoin output jumps 49%, but mining revenue still falls

HIVE’s mining revenue fell 7.4% despite higher production, while its AI hosting business grew 71%.

Bitcoin miner HIVE Digital Technologies produced 49% more Bitcoin in the September quarter than a year earlier, but mining revenue fell as lower Bitcoin prices offset the increase in production.

According to its latest regulatory filing, the company produced 1,070 Bitcoin in the three months ended Sept. 30, 2026, up from 718 a year earlier. Mining revenue fell 7.4% to $76 million as the average price of Bitcoin fell to $71,003 from $114,382 in the prior-year period.

The weaker revenue came alongside a rising power bill. Electricity costs increased to $45.6 million from $37.9 million as HIVE expanded its global mining capacity. HIVE also recorded a $31.2 million noncash write-down on mining equipment, citing “depressed mining economics.”

HIVE’s results add to signs of pressure on Bitcoin mining economics. American Bitcoin last week reported weaker mining margins despite a treasury-driven quarterly profit, while Core Scientific said it plans to leave mining entirely by year-end. HIVE is pursuing a different approach, expanding its AI computing business while continuing to invest in Bitcoin production.

In the latest quarter, HIVE’s HPC hosting revenue rose 71% to $8.8 million, accounting for 10.4% of its quarterly revenue.

Related: American Bitcoin’s $126M profit masks mining pressure as rivals turn to AI

HIVE said it anticipates spending up to $493 million over 36 months to expand its HPC operations, including acquiring land, building out data center infrastructure and buying up GPUs. The company reported spending $145.3 million toward that expansion as of the filing.

HIVE also plans to spend about $35 million converting its Toronto facility into a Tier-III data center. Meanwhile, it continues to expand its mining infrastructure, targeting a 100-megawatt substation expansion at its Yguazú site in Paraguay during 2026.

HIVE’s Nasdaq-listed shares fell 1.7% to $1.78 in after-hours trading Monday, according to Google Finance, after closing the regular session up 1.7% at $1.81.

The company reported fiscal second-quarter revenue of $84.9 million, down 2.7% from a year earlier but above the $79.4 million consensus estimate listed by MarketBeat.

Meanwhile, MarketBeat reported an adjusted loss of 15 cents a share on its analyst comparison measure, beating expectations for a 16-cent loss. HIVE’s GAAP net loss was $78.7 million, or 29 cents a share.
